NFL Week 12 most important games for playoff picture: Rams-Packers, Panthers-Bucs key duels in NFC

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The term "feast week" has long been associated with college basketball, but this week, the NFL is rolling out a spread unlike any seen before. It hasn't even been that long — Week 17, 2024 — since the last NFL week with games on five different days.

But this specific combination of games on Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Sunday and Monday?

Never, ever before, according to CBS Sports Research.

If the last few weeks have felt like duds, this week will feel like drinking water from a firehose, and not just in terms of quantity but quality. With all 32 teams in action and several games between teams vying for a playoff spot in the same conference, the playoff picture is bound to get a major makeover. According to CBS Sports' forecasts and projections, 12 teams could see their playoff leverage swing by at least 15% depending on their outcomes. These are the five most important contests for the playoff picture.

Monday, 8:15 p.m. on ESPN (Stream: fubo, try for free)

The Panthers are already two games clear of every other team in the division, but this is Carolina's chance to deliver an early dagger, as their drastic swing in postseason chance indicates. Not only would the Panthers be three games up on the Buccaneers in the standings with a win, but they'd also even the head-to-head tiebreaker. A Buccaneers win, though, would draw Tampa Bay within a game, secure the head-to-head tiebreaker over Carolina and push Baker Mayfield and Co. to 3-0 in the division.

A fittingly crucial finale to a crucial week.

The week ends with a bang, but it starts with a bang, too. The Packers currently own the final NFC playoff spot, but the Seahawks and the Rams have the same record and trail only on tiebreakers. It's not quite an elimination game, but the fact that both teams' chances drop so drastically with a loss shows it's close to one.

Sunday, 8:20 p.m. on NBC (Stream: fubo, try for free)

The Browns have been one of the season's biggest surprises, currently sitting sixth in the AFC. Right now, there's some separation between the back half of the AFC playoff field and the teams trying to chase it down, and if Cleveland keeps stacking wins, teams on the outside will continue to look in, unable to draw any closer. An upset loss, though, would open the door some.

Sunday, 1 p.m. on CBS, streaming on Paramount+

Speaking of teams trying to track down the Browns (or, perhaps more likely, the Steelers) at the back end of the AFC playoffs, the Texans have to take care of business here against the Ravens. A Texans win plus a Steelers loss to the Broncos would draw Houston to within a game of Pittsburgh.

Sunday, 4:25 p.m. ET on Fox (Stream: fubo, try for free)

Our projections currently favor the Seahawks most among the three 6-4 NFC teams (Seattle, Green Bay, Los Angeles), but either Green Bay or Los Angeles will get to 7-4 on Wednesday, and Seattle won't want to fall a game off pace. Remember, the Seahawks already blew one critical game against the 49ers, turning the ball over four times in a 32-29 Week 5 loss.

Sunday, 1 p.m. on CBS, streaming on Paramount+

In this series, when we've talked about the playoff picture, we've thought of it as if teams are included, not where.

But the Thanksgiving Day opener, airing on CBS and streaming on Paramount+, is too big to ignore. The Bears and Lions are both playoff shoo-ins, but this will go a long way in determining which one is the king of the North. And remember, it's not just a potential division title on the line, but playoff seeding and home-field advantage, too.

Winter Weather Hits EV Range Hard. One Automaker Handles It Better Than Tesla

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

With the first signs of winter making their presence felt in some regions, it’s handy to remember that electric cars lose range when temperatures drop considerably. It happens across the board, but some automakers and models handle the cold better than others, and a new study of over 59,000 EVs from Recurrent shows which battery-powered cars retain the most range when the thermometer reaches 32°F (0°C).

This year, Tesla has been dethroned in the automaker rankings by an unexpected competitor, though the American EV manufacturer still holds the first two spots when it comes to individual models.

Polestar, the Swedish-headquartered, Chinese-owned company that’s no longer allowed to sell cars in the United States after its inventory dries up, surpassed Tesla in the EV winter range rankings. The company’s models retain, on average, 88% of their range at 32°F (0°C) compared to ideal conditions, and 83% when the thermometer reads 20°F (-7°C).

Meanwhile, Tesla, which has historically been the top performer in Recurrent’s rankings because of its early investments in heat pump systems and other winter optimizations, is second on the list this year, with an 85% average range retention at 32°F (0°C) and 77% at 20°F (-7°C).

Audi completes the automaker podium, with the German marque’s EVs retaining 79% of their range at 32°F (0°C) and 71% at 20°F (-7°C), according to Recurrent.

At this point, it’s worth noting that the comparison point for these figures is not the official EPA range figure, but the longest range achieved by a particular EV in ideal conditions, as recorded by Recurrent’s system. As a reminder, the company gathers real-world data from EVs through owners who are willing to connect their cars to Recurrent’s servers.

This year, Toyota is at the bottom of the list, with its EVs retaining just 70% of their summer range at freezing temperatures, with Mercedes-Benz not far ahead. Rivian ranked sixth, with an average range retention of 78% at 32°F (0°C), ahead of Hyundai and Porsche, but below Jaguar and Nissan.

When looking at individual electric models, Tesla still has the lead, with the heat pump-equipped Model S ranking first. The discontinued electric sedan retains 90% of its summer range at 32°F (0°C), followed by the soon-to-be-discontinued Cybertruck, with an average retention rate of 89%.

Despite Tesla losing to Polestar in the overall rankings, the Model S still has the lead in the models list.

Next up is the Polestar 2, the entry-level sedan that put Polestar on the map. The four-door EV can still deliver 88% of its summer range at 32°F (0°C), followed by the Tesla Model X with 87% and the Tesla Model 3 with 85%.

The popular Hyundai Ioniq 5 is 10th on the list, with an average range retention rate of 81%, below the Ioniq 6, Audi e-tron GT, Tesla Model Y, and Chevrolet Silverado EV, but above the Audi e-tron, Rivian R1S, and Nissan Ariya.

The pre-facelift Toyota bZ4X is at the bottom of the list with 70% of range available when the temperature is 32°F (0°C), ahead of the Mercedes-Benz EQS, Chevrolet Blazer EV, Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV, and Cadillac Lyriq. Interestingly, the Volkswagen e-Golf, which is over two decades old and doesn’t have a battery thermal management system, ranked above Cadillac’s Lyriq.

On average, the 43 electric models included in Recurrent’s dataset retain 78% of their range in freezing temperatures compared to ideal conditions. That said, the best EVs out there top out at 90% range retention, while the bottom of the pack gets only 70%.

A heat pump makes a world of difference when it comes to boosting winter range. Compared to traditional resistive heating systems, an EV equipped with a heat pump will have 8% more range in the winter, which can make the difference between getting home and calling a tow truck.

Speaking of home, home charging and preconditioning the car’s interior before setting off will also preserve precious miles when it’s time to go somewhere with a thick coat on. By using electricity from the grid to heat up the cabin, the battery will last longer before needing a recharge.

Red, White & Royal Blue Returns With Brand-New Sequel

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Red, White & Royal Blue fans have been eagerly waiting for news about Prime Video's sequel movie, Red, White & Royal Wedding, which stars previously hinted could release by the end of 2026. That's now looking unlikely, given that it's nearly December and we still don't have a confirmed release date. But thankfully, fans won't be entirely without new Alex and Henry goodness this year.

Author Casey McQuiston, who wrote the original Red, White & Royal Blue book and co-wrote the screenplay for the sequel movie, has a new book out this December. Better yet, seven years after the original book's 2019 release, McQuiston's latest finally brings fans back to the world of Red, White & Royal Blue. Though the author has released quite a few books since the massive bestselling love story burst onto the scene, many fans have been eager to see more of Alex and Henry's story. And now, McQuiston has finally delivered.

Red, White & Royal Blue: The Private Correspondence is a brand-new sequel novella, set to release on December 1. Though McQuiston was involved in the sequel movie, this book is not a direct novelization of that screenplay or even the same story. So, fans who want to avoid spoilers for the movie don't need to worry about picking up this exciting new installment in the love story between America's First Son and Britain's Prince Henry.

The new novella is the first-ever sequel to Red, White & Royal Blue. It takes something of a different approach to the story, leaning into the texts and correspondence that peppered the original. As the title suggests, The Private Correspondence is an epistolary story, told through the handwritten notes and letters our heroes have turned to after that email leak business. The story spans 20 years following the original, telling the everyday story of Alex and Henry's life together. Alongside their letters, the book also pulls in journal entries to give readers even more access to the lives of our favorite royal couple. And yes, we'll see other beloved characters return in the pages of this long-awaited follow-up to Red, White & Royal Blue.

Though it looks like the sequel movie will probably miss that "late 2026" release window, the book is still on track for a December 1 release. That means fans will be able to grab The Private Correspondence ahead of the holidays, for themselves or the Red, White & Royal Blue enthusiast in their lives. And since this is a new, unrelated story separate from the sequel movie, that means we still have more to look forward to when Red, White& Royal Wedding does finally drop on Amazon Prime.

Of course, it's hardly a highly anticipated new sequel without a special Deluxe Edition, complete with sprayed edges, floral endpapers, and exclusive artwork. The hardcover first print run of Red, White & Royal Blue: The Private Correspondence will offer all of that and more. With a suspiciously high page count for something billed as a novella, it's likely that some full-page illustrations will be in the mix as well. If you prefer a more budget-friendly option, the newest Red, White & Royal Blue book will also be available as an eBook or audiobook. A paperback release hasn't yet been confirmed.

You can pre-order Red, White & Royal Blue: The Private Correspondence here.

Hollywood Gets Roasted in Satirical Picture Book ‘We Make a Movie’: ‘I Loved Your Script … Now Change Everything’ (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

“I loved your script. It is perfect. Now change everything.”

While probably far from a joke for many screenwriters who have had similar conversations, this particular line is taken from upcoming satirical picture book “We Make a Movie.”

Designed to look like a children’s book from the 1960s (particularly the U.K.’s famed “Key Words” educational series by Ladybird), “We Make a Movie” comes from artist Miriam Elia and film industry analyst Stephen Follows and features 20 hand-painted pictures accompanied by short, cheery lines merrily roasting Hollywood and the film industry at large. At the bottom of each are three “new words” to explain what’s really going on (in the case of the script line, the words are “squash,” “original” and “voices”). Related Stories Emma Stone Ambushes Druski’s ‘SNL’ Monologue in Adorable Fashion Inde Navarrette's 'SNL' Episode Hits Season High of 5.2 Million Viewers

“The script says Paris by dawn. The tax break says Eastern Europe,” reads another page likely to make more than a few producers nod knowingly. “‘I loved your film,’ says the sales agent. He has not seen it,” adds another.

“Every page comes from something true,” said Follows, who has published more than 600 data-led studies of the industry and is regularly cited in the press. “Anyone in the business will recognize it, and the people it’s about will laugh along without realizing it’s them.”

Due out next month, “We Make a Movie” has already become something of a hit in the film industry. Pre-orders for the first edition (available to order at www.wemakeamovie.com) are on course to sell out, with a second run being planned.

“‘We Make a Movie’ is the book of the year — it explains the film industry in a nutshell and in the most entertaining and funny way ever,” said Uwe Boll. “I loved it! A lot of truth to it.”

“We Make a Movie” is already the fastest selling book in Elia’s comedy Dung Beetle series since 2014’s art-world satire “We Go to the Gallery,” which prompted Ladybird owner Penguin books to threaten legal action (she eventually changed names and logos and, following a new copyright law amendment, was able to re-release it).

‘The Body’ Star Kristina Bogic Just Wanted to Take Care of Anya: “A Lot of Young Women Can Relate”

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 19-year-old star of Netflix's latest hit breaks down how she came to love her dance team captain character: "Anya always wants to try and do the right thing."

The Body team searched far and wide to find their leading lady, Anya. Kristina Bogic, the 19-year-old who landed the role, knew there were plenty of others trying for the part. “A lot of my friends had auditioned for Anya,” she tells The Hollywood Reporter.

By the time the audition for The Body came her way, Bogic had faced her fair share of rejection, as any actor would. She’d had “hundreds and hundreds” of auditions before, so she had a feeling that there was no way it could be her. Newly 18 at the time, the actress just decided to go for it. And it worked out. Related Stories Business Netflix Buys Radford Studio Lot for $380 Million TV Kevin Kline Boards 'The Corrections' at Netflix

Bogic did four chemistry reads with Sara Boustany, who plays Anya’s best friend and secret romantic interest Elise in the series. The actress had “a bunch” of callbacks — she just kept getting more scripts. “I remember thinking even if I wasn’t in this show, I would just be so excited to watch it,” she says.

The raunchy psychological thriller has become an online favorite. Bogic felt protective over the character from moment one. “I can’t believe I get to play Anya,” the actress says. “I really just wanted to take care of her the whole time.”

Anya’s the captain of her Catholic high school’s dance team. In addition to mass hysteria incidents, the teen’s also dealing with complicated feelings for her best friend. Bogic wanted to keep the character tucked safely under her wing.

“I’m so happy I got to explore her warmth and also her dark sides as we,” she says. “A lot of young women can relate to Anya.”

Below, Bogic digs into finding the character, her relationship with her co-stars and what she thinks happened in that season finale.

What was the hardest part in trying to find Anya?

I remember in the table reads, I was still trying to find the essence of her and who Anya was. We had eight weeks of dance training. I did some acting classes, and I did four hours of dialect coaching every week. These women on this set were so amazing at improv as well. They’re so funny. It came to them so naturally. That’s something I’ve never felt like I’ve struggled with, but on top of the accent, it was like I just wanted to feel so settled in it that I could experiment with whatever.

The moment I cracked Anya was when I had the conversation with Lisbeth at the fire pit. I remember Quinn [Shepard] saying she wants this essence of this cool girl. She’s popular. She’s the team captain. There’s a level of dominance there, but also there’s a level of softness. I think she still has this innocence of the world and this naivety to her, which I think I wanted to protect in this season. It starts getting stripped away from her, from her bad decisions. I do think Anya always wants to try and do the right thing.

Your character’s relationship with Sara’s character is a driving force of the show. How did you find that dynamic?

It was very easy, honestly. The first time we spoke, we had maybe 10 minutes just to chat on Zoom before we did the audition, and we just clicked. We were both equally as excited and passionate about the show, which I don’t know if it’s rare, but I’ve never had that experience with someone on set before. We were all so passionate and so excited to be there. We were so grateful and tried to take in every single moment of it. I think that also just came into our characters as well.

[Anya] and Elise are cheeky. They’re fun. I love that we kind of played [it] off as like, we’re just girls. This is just what girls do. Girls hold each other’s hands. Girls do this. We’re just joking around. Then you realize there’s obviously so much going on underneath. I feel like our chemistry was honestly quite instant. Even just as friends, me and her, we still talk all the time. Our cast is just very close in general.

What was your dancing like when you took this on?

I hadn’t danced in five years before this. And I remember Quinn, after I did one of the chemistry reads with Sara, Quinn asked me to a one-minute improvised dance video. I remember thinking I was just going to film it quickly. I was in a hotel in Italy. I went outside and I was like, “All right, let me just see if I can throw something together. Let me move my body.” I was so awkward. I felt like when a baby deer is just born or a baby horse and they’re wobbling, that’s how I felt. I didn’t realize how important it is to know how your body moves.

It gave me extra confidence. That’s also how I found Anya, was I finally felt comfortable in my body. I knew how it moved and how it flowed. It tapped me into Anya’s femininity, which she really values and uses as a form of currency in her life. We had eight weeks of this bootcamp with Ryan Heffington. He was so amazing. He was so patient with all of us. We’re professional, but we’re also little frat boys on set, that’s how we act. We’re just roughhousing all the time and constantly chatting and asking questions. It was a lot of energy, and he was just so amazing. I’m so grateful that we had him for those weeks.

Did most of you not have a background in dancing? Was it kind of fresh for a lot of people?

No, honestly. I walked in [and] everyone’s a pro-level dancer. I walked into watching Emily Roman doing a front aerial. Everyone had a bit of background in dance, but we all caught onto it very quickly.

What was it like to work with these women

I think we were so lucky with this cast. Can you imagine just putting 12 girls in a room and saying now you all have to pretend to be friends? Not for one second did we ever feel like it was forced. I love every one of those girls to death, genuinely. It was so fun just seeing everyone take their character off the page. We were all there for each other, and everyone was so amazing. They completely made their character their own. There was not one sense of ego on set, not one sense of competition. We really all felt like it was very collaborative and very supportive. I remember telling this to someone in my life and they were like, “Oh, it’s so rare that 12 girls are going to get together. I can imagine the drama.” We had the least amount of drama ever. We were so chill. We literally got to hang out and play dress-up with our friends all day.

Can you walk me through where Anya’s at in that first episode?

She’s at a very desperate and vulnerable place. She’s in fight-or-flight, and I think she also. It’s a common theme for her to repress a lot of things. Whether it’s her sexuality or it’s what’s happened with Grace, what’s going on with Steven, her issues with sex. She’s always repressing something.Even the way she presents herself, she’s like, “If I look different, people are going to know something’s wrong. I can’t hang out with Elyse. She’s going to know something’s wrong.” Everything is just pushed down and suppressed. Me and Anya relate to each other a little bit in this way of things snowball and then we just fall apart a little bit. She’s just in a desperate state, honestly.

What is that moment for Anya in the show that you find to be that moment of falling apart?

She has a little one in episode two, when she sees Grace outside the strip club. But that scene is actually so much funnier than I thought it would. I took it there because I thought it was going to be really dark and sad. I watch it and I’m laughing. They’re so hysterical. It’s so ridiculous and so funny. But episode eight for sure. It’s the breaking point for her. Everything’s just come out, at least within a circle, and she’s been faced with everything. She can’t push anything down anymore. Definitely episode eight.

At the end, do you think she’s confessing because she’s feeling guilty or do you think she’s feeling like she can push off what’s actually happening again?

I think this is genuinely how she’s feeling. When she confesses… I think the reason why she can’t admit a lot of things is because of this Catholic guilt and this shame. She always wants to do the right thing, so I think when it comes down to it — although maybe it’s the wrong execution sometimes — it’s almost like she had lost this innocence and this softness to her. That’s really what she’s asking for, she wants herself back — I want my life back. Nothing is going to be the same after this. How could it? That’s really the plea that she’s making, deep down, is I want my life back. I just want everything to go back to normal. I wish I could just repress this. Once Grace wakes up, she’s like, “Oh my fucking God, it’s coming for me. There’s no outrightness anymore,” and I think she just has to face what she’s done.

You May Qualify for Child Care Assistance – Some States Help Middle-Class Families

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

If you work or attend school or job training, apply to your state’s child care assistance program even if you think you earn too much, and do it early, since many states keep waiting lists. For a child under 5, apply to free Head Start or Early Head Start as well.

Every state runs a child care assistance program with money from the federal Child Care and Development Fund, and each sets its own income limit, with some limits well into the middle class. Vermont helps families earning up to nearly six times the federal poverty line, and New Mexico has no income limit at all.

Federal rules cover children under 13 whose parents work or attend school or job training (under 19 for some children with disabilities or under court supervision). You choose a provider that takes the subsidy, the program pays the provider, and you pay a copay, your share of the bill, which your state sets by income and family size, sometimes at zero.

Picture a single parent approved for her state’s program, which pays her day care everything above her copay (figures illustrative). Earning $40,000, she pays $200 a month toward a $1,100 bill, so the program saves her $10,800 a year. Her approval lasts at least 12 months, and a raise in that time can end it only by lifting her income past 85% of her state’s median income.

Head Start and Early Head Start are free. Head Start serves mostly 3- and 4-year-olds, and Early Head Start serves infants, toddlers, and pregnant women. Families qualify with income below the federal poverty line or with cash welfare (TANF), Supplemental Security Income (SSI), or SNAP food benefits, and children in foster care or experiencing homelessness qualify at any income. Programs can fill up to 10% of spots with families above the line, and some can fill more with families under 130% of it. For a 3- or 4-year-old, check whether your state offers free public pre-K too.

The National Women’s Law Center counted over 400,000 children on child care assistance waiting lists by fall 2025, so apply now.

To apply, pick your state on Childcare.gov and open its financial assistance page, which links to your state’s program. For Head Start, enter your ZIP code in the Head Start Center Locator.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tottenham Hotspur target Hakan Calhanoglu would prefer to stay at Inter Milan rather than move elsewhere next summer, sources have told Football Insider.

The midfielder is out of contract with the Italian giants at the end of the season, but has already entered preliminary talks over a new deal.

However, that hasn't stopped Spurs from showing an interest in a potential free transfer next summer, as well as other Serie A clubs and teams in Turkey.

Sources say that Calhanoglu's "first choice" at the moment is to pen a new deal with Inter, to prolong his stay at the San Siro.

The 32-year-old is understood to be happy and settled in Milan, where he remains a key player for Inter in all competitions.

Calhanoglu notched 19 goal contributions from just 30 games last term, and already has three goals from his opening 10 league games in 2026-27.

Tottenham are long-term admirers of Calhanoglu, but they may be about to see another opportunity to sign him pass them by.

The Lilywhites face an anxious wait to see whether the veteran can reach an agreement over a new deal at Inter, knowing that their chances of swooping in will end if a deal is done.

Spurs are not prepared to pay a fee for the Turkish star at this stage of his career, but would pounce on the opportunity to bring him in for free.

However, Calhanoglu has a strong reputation within European football, and top clubs from across the continent are expected to crowd around him if he becomes available.

For now, there is little Tottenham can do other than sit and wait for the outcome of the contract talks, but it could quickly become a race to a deal if Inter cannot reach an agreement.

Former Tottenham scout Bryan King told Football Insider earlier this week that Luca Williams-Barnett's emergence at Spurs may prompt Mikey Moore to explore a permanent exit next summer.

Williams-Barnett notched a goal and an assist last weekend against Ipswich in his first ever Premier League start, while Moore is struggling out on loan with FC Koln.

King pointed towards the fact that the pair both play in similar positions, and he already feels that Williams-Barnett is a more accomplished player than Moore.

Williams-Barnett's career has blossomed in recent months, and that may potentially have a detrimental effect on Moore.

“Do You Like It? Would You Wear It? Would You Buy It?”—An Oral History of Marc by Marc Jacobs

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In the 2000s, Marc Jacobs was the high-low king of fashion. A decade after he propelled grunge onto the runway with his landmark collection for Perry Ellis, he was the first creative director at Louis Vuitton, and LVMH owned a majority share of his business. Meanwhile, he was selling $2 condoms in matchbooks alongside wares in every category: rarefied lace-trimmed cocktail dresses for the well-heeled, shrunken denim military jackets for downtown girls, and of course, those ubiquitously irreverent tchotchkes on Bleecker Street for everyone in between. Ineffable cool across all price points was the name of the game, especially with the launch of Marc by Marc Jacobs in 2001, which loomed large over the booming contemporary market of the era. It was so Kirsten Dunst. It was so Alexa Chung. And at the same time it was so me circa my first trip to NYC with a five-dollar lipstick pen and not much else in a branded shopping bag.

Behind Marc by Marc was a small team encouraged to follow its instincts, with creative direction by legendary stylist and photographer Venetia Scott, who had been consulting on the main line since 1997. Together, they trawled vintage shops and film costume warehouses for inspiration. But, perhaps most of all, there was Robert Duffy, the shrewd fashion executive who was Jacobs’ co-founder and longtime consigliere, tasked with turning the designer’s whimsical runway impulses into dollars and cents. They were the Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé of a new generation, and together they built a company that was both creative and commercially viable. Introducing Marc by Marc Jacobs was a perfect example of their dualities coming together to produce harmonious results.

And, oh, the stores. Jacobs’s shops helped transform a once sleepy stretch of the West Village into a destination, with six outposts within walking distance of each other. Among them was the Marc by Marc Jacobs accessories store, the pièce de résistance for tourists and broke fashion kids alike. Whether it was your first trip to New York or your tenth, you lined up on Bleecker. Tote bags came stamped with the most iconic run-on sentence in fashion history: Jacobs by Marc Jacobs for Marc by Marc Jacobs in Collaboration with Marc Jacobs for Marc by Marc Jacobs. With a simple tongue-twister, Jacobs let us all in on the joke.

Marc by Marc Jacobs was folded into Marc Jacobs in 2015, but it lives on as a moment of accessible cool that can’t be replicated. For those who worked on it, its magic was in the freedom they were given to create. Twenty-five years after it was first launched, we spoke with members of its creative team about the vintage hunts, the last-minute fittings, and how they were able to recreate the downtown cool around them for the runway.

In a serendipitous turn of events, Vogue learned that there are plans underway by to revive the Marc by Marc Jacobs label, sometime next year. While details are currently under wraps, this is the place for updates on the beloved brand that had an outsize imprint on fashion in the early aughts.

Lauren (the Marc by Marc fit model), Frederic Tremblay, and designer Susanne Deeken.

Backstage at the London show held at Claridge’s.

Venetia Scott began working with Marc Jacobs on the main collection in 1997. By the time Marc by Marc was taking shape, she had become a central creative collaborator and operated as the creative director on a day-to-day basis with the team.

Venetia Scott, Creative Director (2001-2015)Joe McKenna had been [styling] the Marc Jacobs runway. He suggested that I would be good to take it over, and Nian [Fish] from KCD also thought so. I had just had my daughter about three weeks before, and had gone down to Cornwall. And then my agent rang and said, “Will you go and meet Marc Jacobs and Robert Duffy?” They ended up coming to London, and then that was it. I started that September. When I was assembling my team, I brought Alister Mackie to style men’s. Frederic Tremblay and Aude Tabet were already on the team. Fred was focused on the army aesthetic and denim. Susanne Deeken came in to do summer dresses. Irene Chung did the shoes.

We needed accessories for the show. I knew Katie Hillier from London, and she had been doing necklaces and stuff for Luella’s shows. So I got her to come out to make stuff, strings and beads. Eventually we’d put a small run in the Marc by Marc shop, and then later, it grew into bags.

Alister Mackie, Menswear Consultant (2003-2014)I started on the Marc Jacobs main line around 2001, when they showed men and women together. In spring 2003, we started putting men into the Marc by Marc show. Venetia would set the narrative. At the first meeting, she would have spoken to Marc, and this story of who these kids were would be laid out by Venetia. We would then have to go off and find a boy that was relevant to that. This was really driven by character, which made it unusual, and it made it a lot of fun.

Venetia ScottThere was not really a strategy behind a lot of it. It sort of evolved as it went along. [Co-founder] Robert Duffy was very much like, “Let Marc do what he needs to do, and he will do it.” We never hit our deadlines. I remember Marc saying, “It’s great, we do what we want to do, and it seems like people like it.” I think we were lucky to be able to work in that vacuum without a lot of people dictating what the needs were of the line.

Frederic Tremblay, Senior Design Director (1999-2008)LVMH was seeing the contemporary market brewing. Our price point was a fraction of the main collection, but we took the essence of what Marc was. There was this big opportunity for department stores, who were also telling us, “We’ll give you tons of square footage because this is what we’re seeing as the next thing, but there’s nobody there.” Co-op was happening at Barneys, so we were one of the first big brands.

Backstage at the spring 2006 Marc by Marc Jacobs show.

Venetia ScottMarc was the author of the collection, and so we would have conversations, decide where we were going with it, and then I would work doing the research on fabrics or prints. And then I would go to Paris or to New York, and take him the research that I had done. Marc by Marc was a little group of us, and it was much more like a collaboration.

Camille Bidault Waddington, stylist (2000-2014)Venetia was styling the Marc Jacobs show and was working on the Marc by Marc collection through the season. I was coming for the last two weeks to style the show with her and the team. I was doing looks, but she had the final word. Sometimes it was a fight, but a gentle one. Marc was obviously coming to see us, also to have a breather in the middle of the sophisticated [main collection] prep. We were the kids.

Frederic TremblayMarc would review the collection ahead of the show and suggest pieces we were missing. It was always a great little thing that maybe when we were in the moment, we weren’t necessarily thinking about it, but he would come out and be like, "You know, you need this now." And we were looking at it, we were like, “Yep!”

Senior design director Frederic Tremblay and Venetia Scott trying on clothes during a research trip in London.

Venetia ScottWe’d go to L.A. for the Rose Bowl Flea and the costume houses, or we’d go to Paris, Antwerp… We’d choose a different place every season. Our thing was always, as a group, “Do you like it? Would you wear it? Would you buy it?”

Camille Bidault-WaddingtonAt the beginning, we were customizing stuff to accessorize the looks, and invented crazy bits and bobs with Katie Hillier, or used things from markets and punk shops. It was very, very free.

Frederic TremblayWe amassed these amazing vintage pieces, and then we had a model coming in to try on pretty much everything. We started doing a bit of a look with the vintage, and that was our starting point. Those amazing costume rental places are truly magical. You’ve got to see Home Depot-size warehouses where you have to go on those industrial ladders, and there’s three, four rows of clothes, rows and rows. I think we just let our gut tell us where to go in the aisles.

Aude Tabet, Design Director and Director of Product Design, Little Marc Jacobs and Marc by Marc Jacobs Swimwear (1999-2015)We reworked prints, color, and artwork based on the vintage pieces we’d found, either internally or using a print studio. I still go by the Marc by Marc color palette we developed. It’s ingrained.

Backstage at the fall 2005 Marc by Marc Jacobs fashion show.

Venetia ScottIn the beginning, it wasn’t an idea to do a cheaper line. It was, let’s make some things that we all want. Marc’s always liked that high and low mishmash of things. Marc and Sofia Coppola were good friends, and other people around him would do this good mix of wearing the bouclé jacket with a silk shirt, with a pair of jeans, with a pair of flats. So that was natural.

What was great was building this gang of cool girls. One might be in a jumpsuit, one might be in jeans and a T-shirt, one girl’s in a summer dress, but they all look like the same gang.

Camille Bidault-WaddingtonThe point was to represent what cool girls would wear. Models at the time had supercool style, and we were inspired by them, from Kirsten Owen to Angela Lindvall and Tasha Tilberg. They were super-stylish, and we wanted to capture the New York streets too—the musicians and the It girls.

Alister MackieSometimes it’s quite hard to fit the men into a women’s story, but it was a fun challenge to make it believable that these girls and boys were together. Fall 2007 (above) was these sorts of winter college kids who were also really grungy. It was very much that character that we kept going back to.

Camille Bidault-WaddingtonSince I’m French, sometimes I thought we were a bit much, but I was then going downstairs with Alister Mackie, who was styling the boys, and actually noticed people in New York were pretty daring. So that was the kick to go back up and add a hat here or a triple sock there.

Alister Mackie[Marc Jacobs HQ on] Spring Street was like the kingdom, and everyone was always there. There was a whole floor of ladies sewing, like a factory, so loud and intense. The whole thing was vibrating. Military coats were getting made up to the last minute, all this food arriving, and all these shoes in the corridors.

Irene Chung, who did the shoes, would fly from Italy with batches. Then she’d fly back and get more because Marc wanted to see all the options, and everything had to be just so perfect. It was this very theatrical experience to be part of.

Aude TabetOur first show was for spring 2001, but our first fall show (above) was my favorite: Dark denim, the coolest girls, slightly English styling, and very Bohemian. It felt almost like the American version of Katharine Hamnett [the English designer known for her mix of tailoring and a vintage-inspired look that was revolutionary at the time].

Camille Bidault-WaddingtonThe music was very experimental, indie, and daring. Frédéric Sanchez was coming up with upcoming bands or weird choirs or film soundtracks. And we were blasting them. Some journalists got annoyed, but it was very fun.

For fall 2007, the brand presented its collection at London Fashion Week. Here, behind-the-scenes shots from the show, held at Claridge’s Hotel.

Another backstage shot from the Marc by Marc Jacobs fall 2007 collection.

Marc by Marc Jacobs’ legacy lives on in some of its most ubiquitous products, like the Moonboots and the Souris mouse flats, first launched in 2002 and soon a cult favorite that would live on for decades and have recently found a new life with a new wave of whimsy enthusiasts.

Hillary Duff seen wearing the Marc by Marc Jacobs signature mouse slipper in 2008.

Venetia ScottThe original mouse shoe is a pointy one, which was done for the Marc Jacobs collection around 1998 with a [bow] that potentially looked like whiskers. And so Marc called it the mouse shoe. The more literal mousey thing with whiskers is what it later became.

Tote bags emblazoned with “Jacobs by Marc Jacobs for Marc by Marc Jacobs in Collaboration with Marc Jacobs for Marc by Marc Jacobs” became another signifier of Jacobs’s tongue-in-cheek approach to the brand.

Frederic TremblayThat was Marc realizing the repetition of his name and the number of lines was getting a little silly. It was just something, kind of, funny, you know? Marc has that ironic thing. He just came up with that, him and Robert.

Amid the designer denim boom of the 2000s bolstered by the likes of Diesel, True Religion, and 7 For All Mankind, Marc by Marc denim launched alongside the first collection for spring 2001. The following February, Vogue reported that its creased jeans were flying off retailers’ shelves.

Aude TabetWe did a creased pair of jeans for our denim launch. The jeans were creased as if they had been folded always on the side, like this forever. That was what we called our “San Francisco SF” wash. It was a mid-pale blue with a gray cast, developed in L.A. And we did all those drummer boy jackets with it.

The main collection and Marc by Marc shared a photographic and visual language, shaped by photographer Juergen Teller and Scott, and later Mackie.

Venetia ScottRight at the beginning, when I worked on Marc Jacobs, Juergen and I were together. Juergen would come over and stay in New York. The conversation came up that there wasn’t any budget, but “maybe when you go back to London, you could photograph Kim Gordon?” And “Venetia, you could take a dress, and you could go to the Shepherd’s Bush Empire, where she’s performing, and she’ll put the dress on,” and “Juergen, you take some pictures of her up on stage or backstage in the dress.” It was just a conversation that didn’t ever really go through lots of tiers of people to be able to get any permission. We just did it.

Early on, there wasn’t any money for those ads. It was just something that we wanted to do. And then it carried on like that, really, as a conversation of who it could be, who’d be a good person this time. Sometimes Juergen would come up with an idea [or see a new actress], like, “Who is she? Oh, I think the girl who’s just been in that film.” And it would be Samantha Morton. Or Dakota Fanning, who I’d just seen in a film when she was tiny, and thought she was the most amazing thing. “Maybe we could shoot her?” We’d just say, “Yes, let’s do that.”

Often Juergen would go off and do the Marc Jacobs campaigns on his own. And then Marc by Marc, we usually used who we thought had been the best girls in the show, or who seemed the most right for it. It’d be maybe three or four of those girls, and they’d often do the shoots. It was more models that had been used on the runway.

Alister MackieSometimes I went with Juergen on trips, and they were phenomenal. We were going to Milan, to Venice, to upstate New York, and we went on these very, very lo-fi trips with him and the models.

In 2010, the New York Times called Jacobs’ West Village retail footprint a monopoly. In other cities, from L.A. to Lisbon, the stores felt like anchors of cool, devoted to a high-low mix that epitomized fashion at the time. Under Duffy’s leadership, Jacobs’s world opened up to the masses via the accessible “junk” operation: an ever-changing assortment of novelties for people who couldn’t afford the clothes.

Aude TabetI moved to the West Village in ’92. The Mercer Street store was the nucleus—this crazy, beautiful, minimal location in that former garage. You would just want to be there, sit in a corner, and people-watch. Before Marc was there, Bleecker was very sleepy. There were a couple of antique stores. Then Marc went there and ended up opening a few stores, and that changed all of Bleecker. Every place where Marc went and opened up retail, everyone followed.

Frederic TremblayA lot of the ideas for the trinkets came from Fiorucci [the popular 1970s brand and store known for carrying an eclectic mix of high-end clothes alongside novelty trinkets and small gifts and where Jacobs worked as a teenager]. Marc would often talk to us about that reference. He would go to the Fiorucci store by Bloomingdale’s, and they had a lot of that stuff too—products that were satellite to the main brand of clothing or accessories, those little things that anyone could afford.

Alister MackieWhat we made was really for the show, but Robert and the merchandising team made all these products, which were really fun and successful, and then they put them together in the stores. Somehow, one elevated the other, and one made the other more fun. The people who worked in the shop represented Marc as much as we did, or the people in the office and the models. Everybody had the same kind of positivity and welcoming spirit.

Camille Bidault-WaddingtonIt was very, very cool to sit on the bench at Balthazar and see so many people walking by, wearing Marc by Marc. It was very cool because it was a collection on its own, not a variation of the other line. It was deep, not just a commercial version.

Venetia ScottYou’ve got your little haul of a tote, and this and that and the other, all for under 50 bucks. It was a fun idea, a bit like Biba in the ’70s, where you could get a T-shirt for very little money or buy a poster to stick up in your room at school. But I think the cheap products that were Marc by Marc, in the long run, probably didn’t do us any good. What we were doing was something very different. We were doing boots that you could sit on the pavement, kick about, and have fun in. They’d get better and better. It wasn’t meant to be a cheap version of something expensive. It was meant to be what it was.

Alister MackieMarc threw these huge parties, and it was very early on for that kind of branding, having these great parties and everybody wanting to be part of it. Chloë Sevigny and all these fabulous women were around—all that was part of the magic of the brand.

Marc’s parties were the talk of the town, and he took it to the extreme. In Venice, he was a pigeon. And then the Arabian Nights, he was camel toe. He took it to this other kind of level of surrealism.

Those parties were also part of the myth of the brand, because everybody was going. It wasn’t exclusive. Everyone in town wanted to be there. That was part of the expansionist generosity of Robert and Marc at that time.

Heidi Bivens, Justin Theroux and Marc Jacobs’ then-boyfriend Lorenzo Martone front row at the spring 2010 Marc by Marc Jacobs show.

Rachel Zoe, Lorenzo Martone and Brad Goreski at the spring 2009 Marc by Marc Jacobs show.

By 2013, the contemporary market that Marc by Marc Jacobs had helped define was crowded with competitors, including Alexander Wang, 3.1 Phillip Lim, and Rag & Bone. That year, as Katie Hillier and Luella Bartley were appointed to lead its next chapter, Duffy acknowledged that the line had lost some momentum. “The competition has become too great,” he told WWD. “We rested on our laurels, and it hurt us.”

Venetia ScottWhen it got really big, then we were asked to do things like, “Oh, somebody’s got, you know, this brand’s got some [legging trousers called] treggings.” And we’d all think, “Well, we don’t want to do treggings.” And I think that was, for me, that was sort of maybe where it lost its way a little bit, and the identity was not as clear as it had been in the beginning. Before, when we were just doing what we wanted to do, that seemed to sell well.

Frederic TremblayAround 2006, 2007, Topshop became a thing. Zara and H&M started to get more momentum. They took a lot of our market. We started feeling like, okay, we really need to offer something different. We weren’t the only ones in contemporary to feel that. We couldn’t really compete.

Venetia ScottOther brands could do that army jacket in a second, and have it out in the shop at the same time that we would. So I think all that contributed to it probably not having the sales that it had once had. I think it was time for a refresh. Katie and Luella came in, and I continued with them to consult on the process. They set up a London studio, and we all worked together on the show and the styling.

In May 2013, Katie Hillier was named creative director of Marc by Marc Jacobs, with Luella Bartley joining as women’s ready-to-wear design director. Their debut for fall 2014 brought an edgier, London-inspired vision to the brand, but it didn’t last long. In March 2015, it was announced that Marc by Marc Jacobs would be absorbed into Marc Jacobs, bringing contemporary and luxury products together under one name. “We believed that fashion could exist at lots of different prices,” Jacobs told WWD at the time.

Beginning with spring 2016, the consolidated label incorporated lower-priced pieces alongside its runway collection, a strategy Jacobs later acknowledged “didn’t really work.” Given the extensive retail footprint of the Marc by Marc Jacobs line, rather than the main line, much of its exposure was lost to customers, leaving space for other brands to replace it.

Venetia ScottThe [pieces] have a character that you don’t find if you go to a high street store. They still are around, what is it, 20 years later? They still look great.

They had all these extra details that you discovered as you wore them. You’d have a piping on the inside of the cuff, which was some little candy cane stripe. They were quite honest clothes, I think.

Aude TabetI wear those pieces from time to time, and I love the emotional connection. I love how disruptive it was. In a good way, positive, disruptive.

Frederic TremblayI’ve been doing mentoring at SCAD, and there are students who will have picked something in a vintage store that’s Marc by Marc. They kind of know what it is, but they don’t really have the whole story about it. They don’t know about the real impact it had when it was made. So it’s always fun when one of the students wears one of those pieces, and I can tell them a little bit about it.

Germany’s Best-Kept Secret: Winter

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nearly everybody visits Europe in summer. That’s no exaggeration: Half of all international inbound arrivals to the continent happen between June and September, with hotels, trails, attractions, and city streets swelling with nearly three times the people you’d encounter in January.

That’s good news for winter fans. Seasonal highlights like festivals and craft markets and storybook scenery give winter travelers a whole different world to discover. Thanks to Lufthansa’s daily flights to its German city hubs from 18 U.S. cities coast to coast, enjoying the holiday traditions and empty museums requires very little effort. Just say yes.

Here’s how to get started on planning your winter getaway.

Frankfurt’s winter markets are best enjoyed on foot, as you’ll wind through more than 230 stalls. Fuel up for the walk with a Bethmännchen, the city’s traditional marzipan pastry, and warm up with a mug of hot apple wine before tracing your way along the Main River. Explore the famous Palmengarten glasshouses for a botanical escape to tropical, desert, and alpine environments, all within the urban core. Beyond the city, enjoy winter silence and peace on hundreds of marked winter trails crisscrossing the snow-dusted Taunus woods.

See why this iconic city deserves its reputation as the international capital for modern culture. Stroll from the treed paths of the Tiergarten to Berlin’s many bridges (more than Venice). Sculptors and architects have turned all of Berlin into an open-air museum, but when the weather turns, you can duck into the galleries peppering the Auguststraße and Potsdamer Straße. Stay fueled at neighborhood cafés inspired by international cuisines, and join the late-night reveling at concerts and dance clubs. (New Year’s Eve is a can’t-miss holiday.)

Elsewhere in Europe, there’s more to love about winter. In Italy, explore the eye-popping Dolomites, or visit Renaissance landmarks in Florence without the crowds. If you time it right, experience Venice’s Carnevale in late January and early February. In France, the Champs-Élysées Christmas market ends its decade-long absence this season, bringing 200 chalets to the famous Paris avenue. Prefer a warmer winter break? Head to Greece, where you can have Athens—and its ancient historic sites—practically to yourself. That’s easy to say yes to.

Whether you want to explore holiday magic in Germany, visit landmarks in Italy, or enjoy a warmer winter in Greece, Lufthansa gets you there.

The Lufthansa Group is a global aviation group with over 100,000 employees from 164 nations. In 2025, it carried more than 135 million passengers and generated €39.6 billion in revenue. Its largest segment, Passenger Airlines, includes the core airline Lufthansa and other leading European carriers, complemented by Logistics, MRO, IT, and Lufthansa Aviation Training.

Personal Investments • Re: What am I missing (Direct Indexing)

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

To me the main fear is having to do an Annualized Income form for taxes. I did that once, and it was a pain to go find every interest payment, dividend payment, capital gain/loss by month, place those into the proper income quarter, and calculate my income by those quarters. I only have a few index funds and a few bank accounts and it took me 30 minutes. If you have a direct indexing scheme, you may have hundreds of stocks to tally. If your 1099-B breaks out totals by month, that would help, but many only list totals for the year which won't help.

If you go with DIrect Indexing, make sure you understand how estimated taxes and safe harbors work and either avoid estimated taxes or never get to the point where you are doing uneven payments or a large one except in the first quarter.

Another factor which does help for direct indexing is if you have a large IRA or 401K. Once the RMDs kick in, and if you don't need to spend them, you'll be having contributions to taxable which will buy more shares at newer prices.

To me, I'm not sure that direct indexing is worth the squeeze. But if you have punitive capital gains taxes, it may be worth it.

MLB rumors: Corey Seager willing to waive his NTC for Phillies, plus latest on Mets, Tigers, Guardians, more

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The uncertain 2026-27 Major League Baseball offseason is fully underway. The ongoing CBA negotiations and the near certainty of an owner-implemented lockout on Dec. 2 loom over all, and it may indeed mean a flurry of trades and free agent signings leading up to that date. That was certainly the case leading up to the last lockout of 2021-22, and this time around we've already seen some leading examples. Just recently, for instance, the New York Mets landed the biggest fish in the pond by signing ace Tarik Skubal to a record contract. Not long after, the Chicago Cubs fortified their rotation with Trevor Rogers and their outfield with Teruaki Sato, and Freddy Peralta is headed to the Baltimore Orioles. More's surely to come before lockout day arrives.

Thanks to that prospect of a hastened pace, offseason rumors are flowing. Speaking of which, we'll be here each day to round up what's out there. You can find Tuesday's supply just below.

The Philadelphia Phillies are open to moving Trea Turner off of shortstop given that he's now a pronounced defensive liability. Speaking of openness, veteran shortstop Corey Seager of the Texas Rangers is willing to waive his no-trade clause to join the Phillies and upgrade the position for them, NBC Sports Philadelphia reports.

Now 32, Seager will always have health and durability concerns, but he remains a plus defensive shortstop who produces well at the plate by positional standards. Signed to a 10-year, $325 million deal prior to the 2022 season (just before the last lockout, in fact), Seager is owed another $155 million through the 2031 season. The Rangers are reportedly looking to shake things up. The Phillies, meantime, are trying to keep their contention window open. It's a fit both ways, at least in theory. Such a move would likely mean a move to the outfield for Turner, who himself has very little trade value thanks to his ongoing decline and big contract.

The Cleveland Guardians have agreed to a free-agent contract with left-hander Kris Bubic, Sportsnet reports. The contract is for $17.5 million over one year, according to ESPN. Bubic, 29, made nine starts for the Kansas City Royals last season, and after the trade to the Los Angeles Dodgers, he was used out of the bullpen. Bubic has an outlier fastball thanks to its rise and his unusual release point, and he's an interesting "ceiling" candidate in the rotation. However, his substantial history of injuries may mean he's best used out of the bullpen.

The Detroit Tigers have added infield depth by agreeing to terms with Ha-Seong Kim on a one-year, $5 million free agent contract, ESPN reports. Kim, 31, is coming off a rough partial season with the Atlanta Braves, but he has a more palatable OPS+ of 91 for his career. He's been a primary shortstop in recent years and also has significant time spent at second base and third base.

Even after achieving a reunion with veteran starting pitcher Sonny Gray, the Minnesota Twins are still looking to fortify their roster for a return to contention in 2027. The club still hopes to make one more significant addition this offseason and add another player who may not grab as many headlines, the Athletic reports. Atop the wishlist are a first baseman or a second baseman and another lefty reliever.

Even after the splash addition of Skubal, the Mets may not be done adding starting pitchers in the service of a rebound season in 2027. On that front, the Mets may be interested in adding a starting pitcher on a short-term deal for next season, the Athletic reports. That would likely limit them to the back half of the market, especially with so many bigger names reaching agreements.

Fantasy football trade deadline strategy: Stop chasing floor. Chase upside

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It might sound counterintuitive, but with just a few more weeks left in the regular season, it's time to start trading for backups who largely haven't done much this season.

The reasoning here is that while you're dealing with byes and injuries during the regular season, having a WR5 who scores 5-8 points a game can actually be valuable. Five points is, as it turns out, more than 0. But in the playoffs, five points might as well be a 0. You're just not going to win many postseason matchups without being able to hit a ceiling.

So as you enter the last week or two or your trade deadline, you should feel fine about swapping high-floor, low-ceiling backups for bench stashes who haven't done much this year and are nothing but upside. Think backup RBs or WRs who might see more opportunity with an injury in front of them. These aren't going to end up being flashy trades, but if a star goes down and you have the backup, trading for him now could wind up as a league-winning move.

There are no teams on a bye this week, so you should have a full suite of players to debate. Here are 10 fantasy football trade targets for Week 12:

Rookie Emmett Johnson has looked like a league-winner filling in for Walker the past two weeks, which might lower the veteran's value just enough to be able to buy him at the deadline. Johnson has played well enough to earn a consistent role even with Walker back, but it also seems unlikely that the Chiefs shy away from arguably the best running back in the NFL in September and October after he returns. If this mini-dip makes Walker available, you should pounce.

Burden's worst game of the season (two catches for 10 yards against the Saints in Week 11) came at a great time for those wanting to trade for Chicago's WR1. The Bears have an extremely easy schedule for fantasy to end the year and get the Bills, Packers and Lions in the fantasy playoffs (Weeks 15-17). Burden could be a WR1 to end the year and you can buy him as a WR2.

Outside of superstar Jahmyr Gibbs and quarterback Jared Goff, Vaki is the only Lions player to earn more than three carries this season. He has just 162 rushing yards this year, but if Gibbs were to miss any time, Vaki would immediately become a top-10 option at RB.

Remember, we don't care if fantasy players score their points in the first quarter or in garbage time – and Ward has been crushing garbage time in November. With a schedule that ends with the Lions, Colts, Raiders and Steelers, he should continue to get opportunities to throw and close the season with weekly top-10 upside.

Starter Bhayshul Tuten has an ankle injury that he continues to reaggravate, which could leave Rodriguez as not just a goal-line back, but an every-down player in one of the best offenses in the league.

I would not trade anything more than the bare minimum for Pittman, who hasn't played since Oct. 1 with a foot injury. He should return this week, though, and could become a more trusted safety valve for the ancient Aaron Rodgers than rookie Germie Bernard or DK Metcalf.

The road to the playoffs is paved with talented, inconsistent rookie wide receivers who go from spike weeks to obscurity. We've seen it this year with Omar Cooper Jr., Germie Bernard and Caleb Douglas. There's a chance that Williams is the true, consistent breakout of his class, but after nine catches for 122 yards in Week 11, you should see if he can command a solid return at the deadline.

The Texans' rush offense has been a mess for two seasons, while Montgomery had his best game of the year in Week 11 because Woody Marks was out with a concussion. With Marks likely back in Week 12, this backfield could get messy. Let someone else deal with the weekly headache.

Smith is back from a hamstring injury and hopefully has enough name recognition to fetch a return at the deadline. Fill-in quarterback Joe Milton has thrown for less than 170 yards in his two starts this year and we generally do not want to start WRs who have QBs throwing for pocket change every week.

Even if Swift hadn't fumbled in Week 11 – which coach Ben Johnson has shown to not tolerate – the veteran still hasn't finished a game with more than four yards per carry since Oct. 11. Extreme touchdown luck has still made him a quality starter, but with Kyle Monangai finally back from a long toe injury, Swift could see his role really weaken over the next month.

You Can See Everything You Ever Want to See in a Movie in <i><em>You Can See Everything</em></i>

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nathan Fielder’s new Elizabeth Holmes documentary is three hours long. You’ll wish it went on for three more.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

NATHAN FIELDER HAS become one of the greatest arbiters of what is—or isn’t—the truth. Fielder, whose mainstream career unofficially kicked off with his show Nathan for You, a dry, quasi-documentary, quasi-scripted, quasi-spoof of shows like Undercover Boss, has found a way to level up with each project he’s made over the course of his still relatively short career, each step of the way inviting audiences to not only question the reality of what he’s bringing to the screen, but their own stances and ways of thinking about things broadly.

The road for Fielder has included HBO’s The Rehearsal (another often surreal, often Lynchian project that presents as real, but on a subtextual level asks viewers to question whether any of what he’s depicting was really happening as it was shown) and The Curse, a Showtime/Paramount+ series that he starred in and co-created alongside Emma Stone and Benny Safdie. That show was, surprise, surprise, incredibly thoughtful, layered, and, ultimately, surreal when it comes to its underlying themes of sincerity and intention.

In a way, all of this has led Fielder to his latest project, the new A24 documentary You Can See Everything. The subject is a fitting one for Fielder: Elizabeth Holmes, the former Theranos CEO and convicted fraudster who was the subject of books (Bad Blood) and television shows (The Dropout) about her misdeeds. It makes total sense that someone famous for skirting the truth would be of interest for Fielder, a creator who has now spent so much time examining not only the truth, but what audiences might accept as the truth even given some reason not to.

In 2022, Holmes was sentenced to 11 years and 3 months in federal prison. That’s where You Can See Everything comes in: Holmes and her partner Billy Evans invited Fielder (and a film crew, led by co-director Lance Oppenheim) to join them in a home they were renting for complete access during the 34 days before she reported to federal prison in Bryan, Texas, in May 2023. And the film begins there.

And, quite frankly, if you’re considering seeing You Can See Everything, it’s best to leave it there. Fielder gets the access he was promised in the movie, but the joy of the film isn’t only in this exclusive vision of such a notable figure, but how an artist with such a relationship to the truth engages with someone who so notably has, seemingly, bent it. We also get to see Holmes and Evans shot as human beings, removed from any of the prior media circuses that have surrounded them. Fielder and Oppenheim put a fair camera to everything and let the camera do what was promised: See everything.

You Can See Everything has been one of the best-reviewed films of the year, and it lives up to the billing. Just when you think you understand what’s happening and what’s going to happen, you’ll be quickly proven wrong. Just when you think you have a handle on who someone is and what they’re all about… you’ll quickly be proven wrong. The movie is three hours long, which, of course, we know, seems daunting. But by the time it ends, you’ll wish it was going on for three hours more.

Below, we answer some of your questions about You Can See Everything—without spoiling any of the movie’s major twists or turns.

You Can See Everything is now available to watch at home. You can purchase it on Apple TV and rent it on Prime Video—and we suggest doing whichever of those you prefer.

You Can See Everything is, in essence, two movies in one. The first half of the movie is what the trailer and promotional materials for the movie billed: Nathan Fielder joins Elizabeth Holmes (and her new husband Billy Evans) in a home they rented for the 34 days before she reported to federal prison in 2023. This proves to be just as compelling as you’d imagine, as Holmes and Evans are both completely idiosyncratic characters. Moments like when Fielder shows Holmes Abbott and Costello’s “Who’s on First” bit are instant classics.

Eventually Holmes reports to prison in Bryan, Texas, and the movie is not even halfway over. It’s at this point that your eyebrow should raise, and you do the ‘leaning forward in seat’ meme—because this is when You Can See Everything gets truly wild. The timeline jumps forward two hours, and Nathan Fielder gets up to some of his mind-bending, Rehearsal-type business. At this point, we see some people’s true colors, and also meet some other unexpected guests and surprises in the movie. This second part of the movie is what will blow your mind, and leave you thinking long after the credits begin to roll.

You Can See Everything should be an absolute lock for a number of Oscar nominations, including Best Picture. And it should be a strong contender in one of the acting categories for one person whose appearance we will not be spoiling in this story.

Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim are the co-directors of You Can See Everything, and both have been key figures in American film and television culture for the last decade.

Fielder is a Canadian-born actor and director (and childhood friend of Seth Rogen) who rose to fame as the creator and star of Comedy Central’s Nathan for You. He’s dabbled in acting here and there—including a role as a limo driver in Rogen’s underrated holiday comedy The Night Before and in the sensational limited series The Curse—but his real calling has come in mind-bending documentary/comedy series like The Rehearsal. In season 2 of The Rehearsal, for example, Fielder actually got his pilot’s license while exploring the aviation industry. (And that was filmed between part 1 and part 2 of You Can See Everything, which is just bananas.)

Oppenheim, who is only 30, has also established himself as one of the best documentarians in the business. His movies Some Kind of Heaven and Spermworld along with his HBO series Ren Faire are deeply layered portrayals of very real people in very real situations; he shoots these documentaries in styles completely different from anyone else, though, lighting them like cinematic spectacles rather than what you probably think of as a documentary in your mind. (Some of the cinematography in You Can See Everything is unbelievably beautiful.)

Primetime, A24’s film about Chris Hansen and To Catch a Predator that came out last month and starred Robert Pattinson, Skyler Gisondo, and Merritt Wever, was Oppenheim’s scripted feature debut.

It probably helps to have information on Elizabeth Holmes’s case—which we recommend getting from any number of sources: The Dropout was the name of the Hulu series that starred Amanda Seyfried as Holmes, but it was also a well-researched and reported podcast that served as the show’s basis. There’s also an HBO documentary called The Inventor and a book called Bad Blood. Any of this would make for a good source for the crimes Holmes was accused of committing. The movie also includes a very brief recap of her case in the early going—not exhaustive, but enough to get the idea.

It does also make sense to have an idea of who Fielder is, what he does, and how he engages with his subjects. We recommend HBO’s The Rehearsal for that, but it’s not 100 percent necessary to enjoy and appreciate the film.

That’s the question, isn’t it? You Can See Everything explores the idea of truth, lies, perspective, and even documentary filmmaking itself. If it makes the most sense to you to take all of You Can See Everything at face value, then please go for it. If you feel the need to question everything, well, that’s okay too. That’s what makes Fielder’s work so unique and so immersive—you can think about it about 100 different ways, and no understanding or interpretation is wrong. It’s set up to feel like a kaleidoscope. You can understand something one way, but maybe something pops up, and suddenly you can’t think about it that way anymore. That’s the point—and that’s what you’ll think about long after the credits roll.

Andrew Scott on Starring in ‘Elsinore’ and Feeling ‘Very Grateful’ to Be an Out Gay Actor

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Andrew Scott remembers first learning about HIV and AIDS when he was growing up in Dublin, Ireland.

“I would have been incredibly young, but I remember Oprah Winfrey talking about it,” he tells me. “She did quite a lot. She had Magic Johnson. That was a little bit later, but I remember she had Magic Johnson on her show and she spoke about the crisis. She had people who were living with HIV. It seemed kind of far away at the time.” Related Stories ‘PAW Patrol’ Spinoff Series ‘Skye’s Pup Squad’ Set for 2027 Release

Scott is now at the center of the conversation because of his starring role in “Elsinore.” He plays Ian Charleson, a star of the 1981 Oscar-winning “Chariots of Fire,” who starred in a 1989 production of “Hamlet” at the National Theatre in London as he was dying of AIDS.

Charleson never publicly revealed his sexuality or his illness before passing away just weeks after completing his run in “Hamlet.”

Scott came out in 2013 in an interview with “The Independent.”

“I relate to it deeply, and I relate to it as a professional who wants to just do my job and as somebody who feels very grateful to get those opportunities that Ian and his counterparts didn’t,” says Scott, who is considered a front-runner in the Oscar race for best actor. “I feel celebratory that I can look around and that there are out gay actors who are growing in number and not placing us in this insidious heaviness burden about separating actors in that way. It feels really wonderful that we have a lot of out gay actors in the movie in a movie about there not being a lot of out gay actors.”

Director Simon Stone says casting an openly gay actor as Charleson was “hugely important.”

“I think what message that sends to the world is that times have changed, and that we’re not going to apologize for the story that we’re telling here,” he says. “Andrew has had the huge benefit of the pioneering work of so many actors before him, that his career was not disadvantaged at all by being openly gay. He’s probably the first generation of actors who can say that.”

“Elsinore” also stars Billie Piper as Patsy, Charleson’s best friend who took care of him through his illness. Piper remembers Stone being mindful of how heavy that subject matter was. “It was a very sensitive set. Simon was very good at setting a very peaceful environment,” Piper says. “People were in their socks, sort of padding around. It felt like a womb. It felt like a nest. People could be extremely emotional. I think a lot of the crew were very upset watching takes. I think for the actors sometimes you’re so adrenalized that you’re sort of just in it. Reflection is something that sometimes comes later.”

The “Elsinore” cast also includes Olivia Colman as Charleson’s physician, Dr. Margaret Johnson, David Dawson as filmmaker Derek Jarman and Joe Locke as a fellow AIDS patient.

Screenwriter Stephen Beresford’s research included talking to the real “fantastically generous” Patsy as well as many other people who knew Charleson. “Some of the people I spoke to are not in great health now, who are older,” Beresford says. “But they made the effort to come and talk to me because they wanted to honor Ian, and that’s very special.”

As Charleson’s health deteriorates in the film, he develops Kaposi sarcoma lesions on his skin. Scott says he tried not to pay attention to the transfer tattoos that makeup department head Nadia Stacey applied to his skin to duplicate the dark cancerous growths.

“In some ways, a little bit like Ian, I had to try and think, ‘Well, they aren’t there.’ He did what he had to do, but he was determined with getting on with the business of living,” Scott says. “He threw himself into ‘Hamlet,’ and I threw myself into ‘Elsinore.’ It was very important to me to not fetishize Ian’s death, but to really think about his living. If everybody else was playing the tragedy around him, he was certainly looking toward the light and in some ways I tried to mirror that.”

Meanwhile, you can also watch Scott talking about Charleson and the making of “Elsinore” with Oprah Winfrey on the latest episode of “The Oprah Podcast.”

First Look Inside American Airlines’ New Austin Lounge—Twice The Space, An Outdoor Terrace And Skyline Views

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I finally got inside American Airlines’ new Austin airport lounge this week. It’s still a shell, but the covered outdoor terrace is already there, and you can see the downtown skyline from the west end of the lounge. The new Admirals Club will have more than twice the space of the current one, and Austin is just one of the places where American is building bigger lounges. American Airlines should be opening its New York JFK ‘Provisions’ grab ‘n go lounge for Admirals Club members in the next month, complete with barista bar. And we’ll see both the renovated Washington National D concourse and replacement Chicago O’Hare L concourse clubs next year. By the end of next year we should also see the new Austin Admiral’s Club as well. The core and shell space is about to be turned over to American by the airport, allowing them to begin construction work. The current club is small. It was perfect-sized when I moved to Austin, but Austin has grown and overall American Airlines lounge visits have increased 80% over the past decade. Expectations for food have increased, too, and the kitchen facilities are inadequate. American is replacing its current club with a new one at the west end of the airport’s main concourse. In March, American Airlines announced the details of the plan for the new club above gate 35. More than 12,000 square feet (more than double the size of the current club) With an outdoor terrace and views of both downtown and airport operations. Supporting a gate allocation that’s expected to nearly double when the new terminal opens in the early 2030s. I keep looking at the space, waiting for progress, each time I fly out of the airport. I’m anxious for the new lounge! So far there are two renderings of the space that have been made available: Credit: American Airlines Credit: American Airlines This week I had a chance to peek inside the space, see the actual views from the space and the outdoor terrace. When you come up the elevator or walk up the stairs you’ll see the entrance – they’ll be taking down a preexisting wall there, and will install e-gates which are a new club standard (like at the Philadelphia A-West club – that location also has e-gates for the Flagship business class lounge, but that won’t be a standard). The kitchen will be at the far left, back of the lounge when you enter. The full kitchen facilities will allow in-house preparation for upgraded food. Closer, also to the left but nearer the windows will be the bar. At the opposite (west) end of the lounge, you can see out towards the downtown skyline. It gives some perspective on how small Austin’s downtown really is, though it’s far more dense than it was when I first moved here. The lounge’s outdoor deck has covering and shade, and American tells me it will have misters and fans – making it much more usable throughout the year. Living in Austin we eat outdoors much of the year, though the summers can get very hot and there will be days you don’t want to be outdoors between November and February. The former Chase Terrace on the opposite end of the airport lacked covering and climate assistance. Taking photos from the lounge is a bit challenging, because of the screens that the airport installs on the window meant for birds to be able to identify the glass and not fly into the building. Nonetheless, angle the lens just right and zoom past it and your photos can come closer to what you see out the windows (you really don’t see these screens except when you’re up close to the glass). American’s Austin gate allocation goes from 4 to 9 — a 125% increase – when the new concourse opens in the early 2030s. American can use common use gates in addition today. All nine of their dedicated gates will be on the current concourse, near this new lounge. Austin is part of a larger investment American is now making in its clubs. After several years where there wasn’t any growth in its network, and they even closed some lounges, here’s the American Airlines lounge pipeline: Airport Project Timing / status Austin New Admirals Club above the west gate expansion: more than 12,000 square feet, roughly double the current club, with an outdoor terrace.
