American Airlines should be opening its New York JFK ‘Provisions’ grab ‘n go lounge for Admirals Club members in the next month, complete with barista bar. And we’ll see both the renovated Washington National D concourse and replacement Chicago O’Hare L concourse clubs next year.

By the end of next year we should also see the new Austin Admiral’s Club as well. The core and shell space is about to be turned over to American by the airport, allowing them to begin construction work.

The current club is small. It was perfect-sized when I moved to Austin, but Austin has grown and overall American Airlines lounge visits have increased 80% over the past decade. Expectations for food have increased, too, and the kitchen facilities are inadequate. American is replacing its current club with a new one at the west end of the airport’s main concourse.

In March, American Airlines announced the details of the plan for the new club above gate 35.

More than 12,000 square feet (more than double the size of the current club) With an outdoor terrace and views of both downtown and airport operations. Supporting a gate allocation that’s expected to nearly double when the new terminal opens in the early 2030s. I keep looking at the space, waiting for progress, each time I fly out of the airport. I’m anxious for the new lounge! When you come up the elevator or walk up the stairs you’ll see the entrance – they’ll be taking down a preexisting wall there, and will install e-gates which are a new club standard (like at the Philadelphia A-West club – that location also has e-gates for the Flagship business class lounge, but that won’t be a standard). The kitchen will be at the far left, back of the lounge when you enter. The full kitchen facilities will allow in-house preparation for upgraded food. Closer, also to the left but nearer the windows will be the bar. At the opposite (west) end of the lounge, you can see out towards the downtown skyline. It gives some perspective on how small Austin’s downtown really is, though it’s far more dense than it was when I first moved here. The lounge’s outdoor deck has covering and shade, and American tells me it will have misters and fans – making it much more usable throughout the year. Living in Austin we eat outdoors much of the year, though the summers can get very hot and there will be days you don’t want to be outdoors between November and February. The former Chase Terrace on the opposite end of the airport lacked covering and climate assistance. Taking photos from the lounge is a bit challenging, because of the screens that the airport installs on the window meant for birds to be able to identify the glass and not fly into the building. Nonetheless, angle the lens just right and zoom past it and your photos can come closer to what you see out the windows (you really don’t see these screens except when you’re up close to the glass). 13 Night Jobs That Fit Around Your Days – Ask Whether Nights Pay More

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Night and evening work lets you earn extra money without giving up your days, from overnight stocking, security, and caregiving shifts to delivery and tutoring gigs you schedule yourself. Before you take an employer’s night shift, ask whether it pays a shift differential, a premium for night hours, since the federal minimum wage and overtime law doesn’t require one.

These hours suit night owls, students with daytime classes, parents who trade shifts with a partner, and anyone adding paid hours after a day job.

The Department of Labor says extra pay for night work is a matter for you and the employer to agree on, or for a union that represents you. The federal government, for one, adds 10% to the basic pay of employees on its General Schedule pay scale for regularly scheduled work between 6 p.m. and 6 a.m., the Office of Personnel Management says.

The pay below is each job’s median hourly wage for May 2025 from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, tips included. Those medians leave out shift differentials, so a night premium would come on top. If a store pays its overnight stockers $2 an hour more than its day crew, 30 hours of night work a week bring in $60 more than the same hours by day.

These pay by the trip, the order, or the tutoring hour, and you choose when to log on. The driving numbers come from Gridwise, an app drivers use to track their earnings, for the third quarter of 2026, and they’re gross pay, before your gas, car wear, and taxes. Work out your hourly pay after costs before you count on one.

If the night job will be your second one, adjust the tax withheld from your paychecks and check your main employer’s rules on taking a second job before the first shift.

A night premium can cover only part of a shift, the way the federal one counts only hours between 6 p.m. and 6 a.m. Before you accept an employer’s night shift, ask what it pays per hour at night and which hours earn that rate.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

Blue Jays signing 5-foot-8 NPB pitcher Kaima Taira after striking out on Tarik Skubal, other FA targets

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

After missing out on bigger name free agent starting pitchers like Tarik Skubal, Trevor Rogers, and Freddy Peralta, the Toronto Blue Jays have fortified their rotation by signing free agent right-hander Kaima Taira out of Japan. The deal is for $80 million over five years with an opt-out after the third year, the New York Post reports.

Earlier this offseason, CBS Sports ranked the undersized Taira as the No. 19 free agent of the current 2026-27 class. Here's part of that write-up:

"Listed at 5-foot-8, Taira gets outlier extension down the mound (close to seven feet despite his height), which creates a low release window. Hitters rarely see the ball come from where Taira releases it. He's not a gimmick pitcher either. Taira sits mid-90s with his four-seam fastball and backs it up with a cutter, sinker, sweeper, slider, changeup, and splitter. The sweeper and slider are distinct pitches, as are the changeup and splitter. The Seibu Lions used Taira as a reliever in 2024 and 2025, then started him in 2026, and he handled the assignment well. The breaking balls are just OK, and the fact of the matter is that pitchers this height don't often hold up under a starter's workload. I would assume the team that signs Taira will give him a chance to start. In the long run, though, he might be a reliever, perhaps even a multi-inning setup man. Seibu announced on Oct. 30 that Taira will be posted this offseason."

For his NPB career, Taira has a 1.85 ERA with 676 strikeouts in 633 ⅔ innings in a mix of rotation and bullpen duty. While, as noted above, Taira may not profile as a starter in MLB, the Jays will likely give him every opportunity to stick in their rotation, given their need there behind ace Dylan Cease. In addition to the $80 million commitment to Taira, the Jays will owe his former team, the Seibu Lions, a posting fee per the MLB-NPB agreement.

Toronto won the pennant in 2025 but backslid in 2026 to 83 losses and a last-place finish in the American League East. The offense was their leading liability last season, but the rotation also needed attention. That explains why they were very much in the bidding for Skubal and other premium starting pitchers on the market. It furthermore explains why they're willing to take a chance on Taira.

Steve Kerr, Erik Spoelstra top list of highest paid NBA coaches

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In 2024, when the Warriors and Steve Kerr agreed to a two-year extension paying him $17.5 million a season, other coaches celebrated — he had driven up the market price overall for coaches.

While other coaches' salaries may have climbed, Kerr remains the highest-paid coach in the league, according to Kurt Badenhausen of Sportico, who did the research on coaching contracts (which are not publicly available). Here are the top 10 highest-paid coaches, all making eight figures a year:

Steve Kerr (Warriors): $17.5 millionErik Spoelstra (Heat): $15 millionTyronn Lue (Clippers): $15 millionRick Carlisle (Pacers): $11 millionIme Udoka (Rockets): $11 millionJoe Mazzulla (Celtics): $11 millionMike Brown (Knicks): $10 millionQuin Snyder (Hawks): $10 millionTaylor Jenkins (Bucks): $10 millionDusty May (Mavericks): $10 million

A few thoughts from the Sportico research:

• Kerr, Spoelstra, and Lue also happen to be the NBA's three longest-tenured head coaches with their current teams.

• Jenkins and May were hired this summer with those eight-figure salaries, with Dallas paying up to get May out of Michigan (where he had just won a national championship). They got hired at higher salaries than established, very successful coaches such as Mark Daigneault in Oklahoma City and Chris Finch in Minnesota.

• The story reports the standard starting salary for an NBA head coach is at least $4 million a season. Two coaches are below that number: Doug Christie in Sacramento, and Micah Nori in Portland, who is on a much-discussed (and despised by other coaches and basketball people) one-year deal with less than $2 million guaranteed, and with options after it. With what Nori has done with the Trail Blazers so far, he deserves a reward of stability (and more cash).

The Best Celebrity Red Wines, According To Our Editors

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These bottles have some serious star power.

Celebrities have taken over the liquor store, with everyone from rappers to actors to athletes launching their own bottles. And the section with the most star power is definitely the wine aisle.

Whether they own their own vineyards or simply slap their names on a label, celebrities sell wines from almost every corner of the world. To see whether any of them are worth the A-list endorsements, we tasted a selection of their most popular red wines. Here are the ones that won over Delish editors.

Cameron Diaz's wine brand, Avaline, specializes in bottles made with organically farmed grapes and minimal additives. Avaline's red blend is made in France with Rhône varietals like Grenache and Syrah.

Our editors appreciated its light- to medium-bodied flavor and bone-dry finish. Notes of red fruit and light spice add just enough depth to keep each sip interesting. And at $24 per bottle, this is a solid table wine that tastes much more expensive than it is.

Reality TV legend Lisa Vanderpump is no stranger to the food and beverage business. In addition to operating six restaurants, she’s also the mind behind her eponymous wine brand. And LVP’s Sonoma County Cabernet Sauvignon is as elegant and sophisticated as she is.

While other Cabernets can be bold and intense, this wine's beauty lies in its subtlety. One editor described it as a “summer Cab” because of its soft, delicate fruit profile. The flavor is complex without feeling too rich or heavy, making it an ideal red for dinner. And at $26 a bottle, it’s a good deal, too.

Famed film director Francis Ford Coppola isn’t just known for his Oscar-winning movies—he’s also a prolific winemaker. He sold his namesake winery to Delicato Family Wines in 2021, but the lineup is still just as strong as it was under his leadership.

We enjoyed all of Coppola’s reds, but the Archimedes Cabernet Sauvignon was a clear standout. Made with California-grown grapes and aged for 20 months in French and American oak barrels, this wine delivers what our editors described as the quintessential Cab, with a full-bodied, smooth flavor and notes of dark fruit and tobacco.

Depending on the vintage and retailer, prices can start around $70 and climb past $100. But if you want to impress both the film buffs and wine snobs in your life, it’s worth the splurge.

Inside Elizabeth Holmes’ Secret Oceanfront Rental Seen in ‘You Can See Everything’ Documentary a Month Before She’s Sent to Prison

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Elizabeth Holmes documentary “You Can See Everything” takes place in the Theranos founder’s former Del Mar, CA, rental—and the property’s location in an exclusive, gated community could be just part of the reason the film’s unexpected release was such a smash.

The project—which is now available to stream on demand and was directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim—was kept secret for the four years it was in production, leaving viewers in awe after the documentary’s surprise Sept. 6 premiere at Telluride Film Festival.

Holmes and her partner, Billy Evans, invited the film crew—which Oppenheim told Variety was intentionally kept intimate to keep the project a secret—to reside in the guesthouse of the home for the 34 days before she reported to Federal Prison Camp in Bryan, TX, on May 30, 2023.

“Keeping it under wraps was very important because we never wanted to get in front of what we were capturing,” the director shared.

The crew would typically shoot for 16-hour days, with Fielder telling Variety that cameras were strategically positioned to “cover the whole house” so nothing would be missed.

The four-bedroom, four-bathroom beachfront property is located in an ultraprivate community less than a half-hour from San Diego. Called Sandy Lane, it features just about a dozen residences.

“This is basically a hidden community. It’s a guard-gated community, which is so rare on a beach, which is why you have a lot of high-profile individuals up and down this strip,” celebrity real estate agent Josh Altman explained in a home tour of another property on the community’s sole street.

Given the home's private location—and considering California’s anti-paparazzi legislation protecting private property from capture—it’s plain to see why Holmes and Evans opted to rent the 2,200-square-foot dwelling in the time leading up to her imprisonment.

The New York Times also previously reported that the couple and their two children moved to the area to be nearer to Evans’ family. His parents, William L. Evans and Susan Evans, are the hotel magnates behind Evans Hotels, a San Diego–based hospitality company.

Although the property is currently off-market, a previous listing indicates the single-story home was built in 1948 and includes direct access to the private beach.

As seen in the film, the abode features tall ceilings with exposed-beams and sizable windows connecting the home to the serene surrounding landscape. Midcentury modern design is present throughout the home, which is paneled in honey-hued wood.

While Realtor.com® estimates the property’s current market value to be about $5.8 million, it’s unclear just how much Holmes and Evans shelled out to reside there temporarily. The most recent publicly listed rent was $5,650 a month in 2015, which is a decrease from the $5,950 rent it was listed for in 2012.

The Sandy Lane spot is not the only prominent rental property Holmes has retreated to since her legal troubles began. During her trial from Aug. 31, 2021, to Jan. 3, 2022, she resided in one of the seven homes of San Francisco’s notable Green Gables.

The sprawling 74-acre property was owned by the Fleishhacker family for over a century before it was sold for a record-breaking $85 million in 2025.

Before Theranos came crumbling down, Holmes and her former business and romantic partner, Ramesh “Sunny” Balwani, purchased a $9 million French provincial manor in Atherton, a wealthy enclave of Silicon Valley.

Privacy was likely a factor in that decision, as well, as the five-bedroom, 5.5-bathroom mansion was set behind a gated entrance and tall trees.

The home was considerably more lavish than the beach bungalow that serves as the backdrop for “You Can See Everything,” with a 6,800-square-foot main home accompanied by gardens, a pool, and a pavilion on a 1.16-acre lot.

Along with the couple’s young children, Evans has since relocated from California to Texas, so the family can visit Holmes in prison, where she is serving an 11-year sentence for three counts of wire fraud and one count of conspiracy to commit wire fraud.

The sentence was reduced to nine years for good behavior, and Holmes has since been approved for placement in a community correction center, ABC News reported, citing an update from the Department of Justice's Victim Notification System.

Holmes is scheduled to be placed in the Austin Transitional Center in De Valle, TX, in August 2027.

She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Tuesday struck a conciliatory and remorseful tone while leading his first employee meeting since being reinstated, and said how sorry he was for what he had done.

Huang told employees that he let them down and promised to do a better job the second time around, according to people inside the room, who asked not to be named in order to share details.

The CEO also addressed succession planning during the event, the people said. He told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.

A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to the person familiar the matter.

Investors and analysts were left to speculate about the company's future leadership in the wake of Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.

On Monday, Huang was reinstated as CEO of the chipmaker by Nvidia's board of directors. Eleven days before, Huang had resigned from Nvidia after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said last week that Huang resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

In a press release announcing Huang's return on Monday, the company said the board "received input" from senior officials in the federal government about any potential national security risk around the CEO. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he gave the board assurances and "made the deal" to restore Huang to his post.

After the company had its worst week in the stock market since 2008, the stock surged more than 30% the day his return was announced. Shares fell on Tuesday, however.

An Nvidia representative didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

Jessica Alba Ordered To Pay Cash Warren $577K—Inside Her Recent Beverly Hills Real Estate Moves

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Jessica Alba’s divorce from Cash Warren is back in the headlines after a new court order awarded Warren $577,481 from Alba’s retirement benefits.

The ruling comes after the former couple sold their longtime Beverly Hills–area home and Alba later purchased another property in the area.

The retirement award comes from Alba’s account in the HonorBar Inc. Profit Sharing Plan, according to court documents reported by TMZ.

Any money left in the account after Warren receives his share is to go to Alba. The documents also allow Warren to take the award as a lump sum or move it into an individual retirement account or another retirement plan.

The court order also addresses account records: If Warren requests statements in writing within the next year, Alba must provide them within 10 days.

According to the outlet, transferring the money into a retirement account could avoid a 20% tax withholding.

The ruling is the latest financial development following the couple’s divorce, which was finalized in February 2026.

It was previously reported that Alba was ordered to pay Warren $3 million to balance the division of their assets.

Their settlement also divided interests in several companies, shares in The Honest Company, and residuals from projects made during their marriage.

Meanwhile, Alba and Warren have already put their real estate ties behind them.

Their former Beverly Hills–area home was sold in 2025. Alba and Warren purchased the seven-bedroom estate for $9.9 million in 2017, then put it on the market in April 2025 for $18.99 million. It was sold for $18.2 million, with the closing recorded on June 27, 2025.

The property sat on about 1.86 acres and included 8.5 bathrooms, a guesthouse, a home theater, and extensive grounds.

Alba and Warren undertook an 18-month renovation after buying it, with Alba involved in decisions about the home’s design.

The estate had been a family residence for years, and Alba spoke about the family’s plans for the house during a 2019 Architectural Digest tour.

After the sale, Alba picked up a splashy Beverly Hills estate in September 2025 for $4.75 million.

The roughly 5,000-square-foot property boasts four bedrooms, five bathrooms, tiled roofing, and modern amenities.

The two transactions mark a change in Alba’s recent housing history: the end of her and Warren’s ownership of a sprawling family estate, followed by her purchase of another Beverly Hills property.

Alba and Warren announced their separation in December 2024 after 16 years of marriage.

Alba filed for divorce citing irreconcilable differences, and the sale of their longtime home followed the announcement.

The latest retirement-benefit order concerns a separate part of their financial settlement, while the Beverly Hills transactions provide a look at the real estate changes that accompanied their split.

Utah asks to drop criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Utah asked a court to dismiss the criminal charges it filed last month against prediction market operator Kalshi and two of its executives, citing Kalshi’s decision to halt trading in the state that had accused it of running an illegal gambling enterprise.

The one-sentence motion filed Tuesday in a Salt Lake City-based court by state Attorney General Derek Brown referenced a “change in circumstance.” Brown’s office noted that it could refile the case if Kalshi resumes offering trades on its platform there.

“Kalshi has pulled out of Utah, and if it returns, so will the charges,” Brown said in a statement.

Utah has been on the front lines of a battle between states and the federal government over who regulates the markets. Litigation has spread across more than 20 states, creating a fragmented legal landscape that analysts say will likely be settled by the U.S. Supreme Court.

The felony charges filed by Utah include 13 counts of violating the state’s prohibition on gambling and another charge involving profiting off illegal activity.

The state’s charging documents name Kalshi and its co-founders, chief executive officer Tarek Mansour and chief operating officer Luana Lopes Lara. They are due in court in Salt Lake City on Dec. 4, unless a judge agrees to dismiss the case.

Utah law prohibits gambling, and Utah considers prediction markets gambling, Brown’s office said. Many other states contend that the platforms’ sports trading activities are flouting their laws that regulate sports betting and casino gambling.

Utah’s felony charges represented a new tactic in how states are trying to assert regulatory authority over the fast-expanding activity. Brown’s office also has asked a court to make Kalshi give up the profits it made in Utah.

Kalshi, the nation’s largest prediction market operator, said Monday it had halted trading in Utah after it became clear that a federal judge hearing a case brought by the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission wouldn’t immediately stop Utah’s attempts to shut it down there.

A Kalshi spokeswoman called it an “unfortunate outcome for the more than 177,000 Utahns who use Kalshi” but said the company will continue to fight in court for the right to operate in Utah.

Kalshi, like the CFTC, argues that prediction markets offer “event contracts” that should be considered swaps under a federal law that grants exclusive regulation to the federal agency. Utah and at least 44 other states have opposed that view.

Kalshi sued Utah in February, asking a federal judge to prevent the state from enforcing its gambling restrictions on the platform. A federal judge rejected the request, and an appeal is pending.

Trading activity on Kalshi is halted in Nevada and partially in Michigan and Washington, according to Kalshi.

—Marc Levy, Associated Press

Consumer Confidence Surges in November

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With the election uncertainty in the rearview mirror and gas prices coming down, Americans’ confidence in the economy surged this month, according to the latest report from The Conference Board.

The nonprofit research firm’s Consumer Confidence Index, which measures American opinions of current economic conditions and their outlook for the next six months, rose by 11.4 points to 103.3 in November, the second consecutive monthly increase after months of glum reports.

The organization’s other monthly indicators—the Present Situation Index, based on consumers’ assessment of current business and labor market conditions, and the Expectations Index, based on consumers’ short-term outlook for income, business and labor market conditions—also both increased, by 13.7 points to 132.9 and by 9.9 points to 83.6, respectively. The survey period for this month’s preliminary results was November 1-17.

“Consumer confidence rallied again in November, potentially due to lower uncertainty following the midterm elections,” said Fergus McCormick, senior economist, Global Indicators, The Conference Board. “After two months of consecutive increases, the headline index is back to levels of confidence last seen in January 2025. The gain in confidence was led by stronger perceptions of present and future labor market conditions. Perceptions of current and future business conditions also improved sharply. Along with the gain in headline confidence, consumers planned slightly higher spending on goods and vacations.”

McCormick said write-in responses in November were slightly less negative than previous months, noting that while consumers continued to express frustration with high prices and rising costs, some comments cited hope for the general economy. He said comments about war and conflict ticked up slightly, along with mixed feelings about elections, politics and the direction of the labor market.

A host of macroeconomic factors continue to hinder consumers who are looking to buy a home. The 30-year fixed-rate jumped to 7.32% as of Nov. 24, fueled by hotter-than-expected inflation in October with a rate hike expected by the Fed in December, as well as homebuilder confidence slumping to a new 14-year low, existing-home sales sinking to a 16-year low and muted new home sales this year have weighed on homebuyers and added more pressure to a typical late-fall slowdown in the housing market.

Yet, despite these indicators, the November Consumer Confidence report also shows on a month-over-month as well as on a six-month moving average basis, plans to purchase autos and homes both improving slightly this month. Planned spending on durable goods in the month generally increased across the board.

Read the full report: https://www.conference-board.org/topics/consumer-confidence/.

BermudAir Adding Flights All Over The Place: I Don’t Know What’s Going On

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BermudAir, the unusual boutique carrier that launched operations a few years back, keeps getting stranger. The company has just announced a massive expansion for the summer of 2027, and with this, we’ll see the airline add all kinds of seemingly random routes to and from Bermuda, plus all kinds of routes that don’t touch Bermuda.

Whether you want to fly from Nashville to Ocho Rios, or from Fredericton to Bermuda, the airline now has you covered!

For a little bit of background, in mid-2023 we saw the launch of BermudAir, a quirky niche airline with the goal of operating Embraer jets between Bermuda (BDA) and the United States and Canada. Initially the airline was going to operate all-business class planes, but it scrapped (“delayed”) that concept before launch, instead introducing a two-cabin concept (though business class just has blocked adjacent seats and better service).

As I said from the beginning, the whole thing struck me as an odd venture, essentially trying to serve Bermuda year-round profitably, while going up against the major carriers that serve the airport.

To my surprise, the airline is still in business over three years after launch, though it hasn’t necessarily been that smooth. As is pretty common for an airlines startup, the first years have been rocky, with several routes being cut after launch. For that matter, the airline faced a lawsuit over millions in unpaid airport fees at its hub, which isn’t exactly a great sign.

Several months back, BermudAir announced new winter 2026-2027 seasonal flying to places other than Bermuda. Now the airline has announced summer seasonal flying for 2027, and it’s arguably even stranger. The routes are all over the place.

In the summer of 2027, BermudAir will fly from Bermuda (BDA) to the following destinations:

On top of that, the airline will be expanding beyond Bermuda, with new seasonal services in the following markets:

Below is BermudAir’s updated map with destinations listed…

I really can’t make sense of what is going on at BermudAir. I feel like we’ve had reasons to be suspicious, when you look at the initial business model, vs. how things have played out:

I’m not suggesting there’s no demand in any of these new markets. The demand may very well be there. But there are a lot of challenges here:

What’s interesting here is how the airline announced seems to be taking a similar strategy both for this winter and for next summer. So based on the fact that the airline is moving forward with its current winter schedule, maybe this is working? I don’t know…

It’s also interesting how the airline actually got permission to operate these routes. As a reminder, Bermuda, Anguilla, and Turks & Caicos, are all British Overseas Territories, and Belize and Jamaica are former British Overseas Territory. How does the airline get permission to serve places like Honduras, though? I guess the airline is somehow getting permission to operate this as fifth freedom service, or something.

BermudAir is expanding rapidly, as the airline has announced new routes for the summer of 2027. Not only does this include several new routes to Bermuda (including out of Fredericton!), but the airline is also adding flying between the United States and Grand Cayman, Honduras, Jamaica, etc.

I think BermudAir deserves an award for the most unusual route network of any “real” airline. I certainly can’t think of any other airline in this “league.”

What do you make of BermudAir’s latest expansion?

Francis Lawrence Signed On for One ‘Hunger Games’ Movie. Five Films Later, He’s the Keeper of Panem

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 'Sunrise on the Reaping' director looks back on the pressure of taking over a franchise, his decade-long collaboration with Suzanne Collins, why the series endures, and where things stand on ‘Constantine 2’ and 'Red Rising.'

The morning after the New York premiere of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, Francis Lawrence is still taking in the view from the night before.

On one red carpet stood nearly every era of Panem. There were the newcomers: Joseph Zada, Whitney Peak and Mckenna Grace, along with franchise first-timers Kieran Culkin, Elle Fanning, Jesse Plemons, Ralph Fiennes and Glenn Close. Tom Blyth, Rachel Zegler and Josh Andrés Rivera who starred in Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes prequel, were in attendance. And there were the originals, Jennifer Lawrence, Josh Hutcherson, Woody Harrelson, Elizabeth Banks, Jena Malone and Willow Shields assembled like the franchise’s own Avengers. Related Stories Movies New 'Hunger Games' Movie on the Way? Lionsgate Film Chief Shares Latest on Franchise's Future Beyond the Book 'Percy Jackson and the Olympians': How Season 3 Brings Epic Moments to Life and Deals With Annabeth's Absence in the Books

“Last night at the premiere, for the first time, we had three generations of cast,” Lawrence tells The Hollywood Reporter of the Nov. 16 event. “There’s something really special about the time we’ve all spent together and the quality of people I’ve gotten to work with.”

Lawrence has been a constant across all of them. Since taking over from director Gary Ross with 2013’s Catching Fire, he has directed every Hunger Games film that followed, working alongside producer Nina Jacobson to turn Suzanne Collins’ dystopian saga following teenagers forced into fatal combat into one of Hollywood’s most dependable franchises. The original four films grossed roughly $2.98 billion worldwide, and Collins’ five books have sold more than 100 million copies globally, growing into one of the most successful young adult publishing franchises in history.

Still, the scale didn’t fully hit him until the final Mockingjay films. Around the release of the films, The Hunger Games: The Exhibition opened to fans, and Lawrence walked through it with his stars.

“Walking through it for the first time with Jen and Josh, and I think Liam [Hemsworth] was there, we were all just stunned that we had made something museum-worthy, with our music, our costumes, our props, pieces of our sets and the lore,” he recalls. “There’s something really special in that, knowing these stories have connected with people in a certain way.”

Amid the success, it’s easy to forget that Lawrence arrived in Panem as an outsider. He had never made a sequel to someone else’s first film, and on Catching Fire he was “joining an existing family,” with a cast “so big and so seasoned” that he felt he had a lot to prove. He also assumed it would be a one-time job.

“The weird thing is, when I signed on to Catching Fire, I thought that was going to be the only one I did,” he says.

Instead, the studio asked him to stay for the two-part Mockingjay. “A huge part of me is very appreciative of being invited to keep making these movies and be part of this team.”

That invitation, however, came with a nerve-racking stretch. A few weeks into shooting Mockingjay, production paused for a month so Lawrence and the team could promote and release Catching Fire. He would face its box office verdict with most of the next film’s shoot still ahead of him.

“Man, what if this thing bombs?” he remembers thinking of the pressure for Catching Fire success amid filming the third installment. “I now still have like 125 days of shooting with all of these people that are going to be looking at me like, ‘We just made a movie with this guy that bombed.'”

He needn’t have worried. After its 2013 release, Catching Fire became the franchise’s highest grossing film, and the once outsider became the keeper of the series. “The series was quite popular at that point, and there was a lot of excitement and confidence at the studio, so that was nice,” he says.

When Lawrence and Jacobson returned eight years later with 2023’s The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes, the odds felt less certain. The prequel had no Katniss and centered on the rise of the tyrant Coriolanus Snow, played by Donald Sutherland in the original films with Tom Blyth taking on the role for a younger version.

“A bunch of time had gone by, and you wonder if there’s still an appetite for Hunger Games, especially Hunger Games without Katniss,” he says. “It had a very different structure, and it was a story about the person everybody hates, so a lot of things made me a little nervous.”

Sunrise on the Reaping, which traces the origins of the fan-favorite mentor Haymitch Abernathy, felt different.

“Weirdly, this one I kind of felt less pressure,” Lawrence says. “I felt much more confident that this was one that fans really wanted to see, and it felt like a return to form for movies like Catching Fire and the original.”

There being a new Hunger Games book came as a surprise to Lawrence, who assures he, along with Jacobson, are just as in the dark as everyone else. After more than a decade of working together, Lawrence and Collins have a routine. She keeps quiet while she writes, and only once her publisher confirms she has a book does she bring in Lawrence and Jacobson, always with the same modest question of whether the story is a movie. Lawrence and Jacobson always say yes.

For Sunrise, Lawrence and Jacobson got word of the novel earlier than ever, and the secrecy was tighter as a result. The manuscript was kept at Collins‘ agent’s home, and then then moved to a locked safe kept with Jacobson. Screenwriter Billy Ray wrote on an offline laptop in Jacobson’s office, while Lawrence stayed busy making secret trips to meet with Collins.

“This one we got further in advance, while she was still going back and forth with her editors,” he says. “I had read it a couple of times, Nina had read it a couple of times, and I went to New York to work with Suzanne on breaking down the book into what we thought the movie would be. We hired [original screenwriter] Billy Ray. He was already writing and we were having calls about the draft. So a lot of work was done before the book was even announced, and I couldn’t talk to anybody about it.”

The book and its film adaptation were announced together in June 2024, which brought “a lot of relief,” he says. “At least then people knew we were working on it and that it was the Haymitch story. We just had to keep quiet about the storyline itself.”

That left the biggest challenge: finding a young Haymitch, the carefree kid from District 12 long before he became the drunken, broken mentor Harrelson made iconic. The search led to Zada, the breakout from Prime Video’s We Were Liars and Netflix’s East of Eden. Lawrence put him “through the wringer” in auditions before landing on the actor to carry the beloved character’s origin story. The seeds for the origin story were “planted over twenty years ago” when Collins began the original trilogy, the author reflected in a letter shared on social media amid the release of Sunrise.

Collins praised Zada’s performance as “remarkably powerful and moving.”

Long before he was steering a multibillion-dollar franchise, Lawrence spent years making some of the most memorable music videos of the MTV era for artists like Britney Spears, Lady Gaga, Justin Timberlake and Jennifer Lopez. He doesn’t think much about those days when he’s making a movie, he says, but their influence is still there nearly 20 years later.

“Having done maybe around 120 videos, it gave me a playground to learn the toolkit I have for making things look a certain way,” Lawrence says. “I got to create different worlds, try different things, learn about visual effects and learn about working with performers. So in terms of visual style, I just had confidence in the image in general: if I imagine something, how can I actually make that happen? Because of videos, that became second nature, so I could focus more on performance and story. That’s probably the biggest way it’s affected my moviemaking in general.”

It’s fitting, then, that music runs through Panem. The films have long used it to amplify their themes of resistance. Jennifer Lawrence’s Katniss sings “The Hanging Tree,” which became a rebel anthem. In the prequels, Zegler’s Lucy Gray Baird and Peak’s Lenore Dove are both musicians who capture the hearts of Snow and Haymitch.

Lawrence approaches that music the way he approached his videos: “much more music-forward than lyrics-forward.”

“I take the mood, tone and feel of a song, and most of the ideas I built for my videos were based on the mood of the song rather than the lyrics. Not always, but I’d say 90 percent of the time. It’s the same here: I’m primarily thinking about the mood, tone and emotional value of music and what I want something to feel like,” he explains.

Music is also common ground with Collins. “She’s a real music aficionado with a library in her head, and I had long conversations with her and with [executive music producer and songwriter] Dave Cobb about the creation of songs. She [Collins] usually has a historical background for why and how she wrote certain things, and I would talk to Dave, our music producer, mostly about how I wanted songs to feel. So whether it’s ‘The Hanging Tree,’ a Covey song or ‘The Old Therebefore,’ it’s really about mood,” he says.

The music doesn’t stop when the cameras roll. “I also listen to a lot of music when I shoot,” Lawrence says. “Often I’ll be watching a scene with music I have as inspiration playing in my ear, whether it’s score from our movies or from other movies, because I’m evoking a mood, a tone and an emotional value I want to feel in the scene. So sometimes I’m listening to dialogue and to music on my phone at the same time.”

His usual choices: The franchise’s scores, of which there are five movies worth, and “Lily’s Theme,” Alexandre Desplat’s piece from Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows – Part 2.

“That has always felt very Hunger Games-ish to me,” he says of the Potter score. “I also listened to some more disturbing stuff for this one, because I thought of it as a bit scarier. Instead of listening to action-oriented music during fight sequences, I’d listen to things that are a little more disturbing and scary. So it was kind of all over the place.“

Building worlds became Lawrence’s calling card when he made the leap to features. His 2005 debut, Constantine, was a dark take on the DC Comics antihero, and he followed it by envisioning a New York City post-plague for the Will Smith hit I Am Legend (2007). Then came the Great Depression-era romance Water for Elephants (2011), a reunion with Jennifer Lawrence on the spy thriller Red Sparrow (2018), and more recently Slumberland (2022) and the Stephen King adaptation The Long Walk (2025).

The genres vary, but in hindsight, the dystopian streak running through several of them looks a lot like a rehearsal for the road to the Panem Capitol. Amid revisiting Hunger Games in the prequels, the director says he’s “trying to” return to where it all began with his first feature, Constantine. It was announced in 2022 that Lawrence would helm a sequel with star Keanu Reeves reprising his role and Akiva Goldsman writing the script. “We have a script,” Lawrence confirms.

Part of the appeal of a sequel is getting the band back together. The other part is unfinished business with the ratings board, and this time Lawrence wants to lean into the R rating he once tried to avoid.

“We got saddled with an R rating even though we followed all the PG-13 rules, and that was a tough blow,” he says. “On Hunger Games, every time I turn in a cut, it’s an R, we go back and forth a little, and we get to PG-13. With Constantine, they said, ‘We’ve stopped taking notes. There’s no getting a PG-13 on this movie.’ So we were stuck. Part of it was: let’s make another one because we enjoyed it, but this time let’s make the hard-R version. If we’re going to get an R, let’s make the R-rated Constantine.”

However, the road to a sequel has been anything but smooth.

“We’ve been trying, and we’ve hit roadblock after roadblock,” Lawrence says. “The character was given to somebody to make a network television show, which I never watched, and I don’t think it lasted long.” He continues, “Then J.J. [Abrams] somehow had the rights, or control of the character, for a while and was going to do something with Constantine. But he switched over, and he’s now involved in our project too. He’s a great guy, and he’s pro making this Constantine movie, which is fantastic.”.

The other obstacle, Lawrence says, is “there were new people running DC with their own plans for the characters.”

Though he doesn’t name anyone, Lawrence notes there has since been a regime change at DC. It was announced on Nov. 9 that James Gunn and Peter Safran are stepping down as co-CEOs of DC studios four years after taking the job. Skydance, which now oversees the division following the acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, has appointed former Marvel exec Jeremy Latcham to the post of president and chief creative officer of DC Studios.

With new leadership in place, and with relationships of his own among the executives now running Warner Bros., Lawrence sees an opening. “Maybe there’s some renewed hope to get Constantine through,” he says.

“I’m a believer that we could make a sequel to our Constantine with Keanu, and if people at DC wanted to do a different version of Constantine in some sort of shared universe, they could do that, and the two could coexist.”

Constantine isn’t the only long-gestating project on his plate. With Sunrise now in theaters, Lawrence is also turning his attention to Red Rising, Pierce Brown’s bestselling sci-fi book series, which he has been developing with the author, a friend, for about eight years.

Development of an adaptation first began as a series at Netflix, with two pilot scripts he calls “fantastic,” but it stalled. “There’s just regime changes, and it was an expensive buy-in for them,” he says. “We kept getting shifted around to different departments.”

“They never really pulled the trigger,” he adds. “Now there’s a renewed excitement because we’re at Paramount and it’s theatrical again. We have a screenwriter working on it, so I’m very excited.”

All roads seemingly lead back to Panem, with Lawrence acknowledging how Red Rising can have a clear thread back to Hunger Games character Plutarch Heavensbee, the Gamemaker turned rebel played by Plemons in Sunrise and the late Philip Seymour Hoffman in the original films. “One of the things that I love about Plutarch is that Plutarch is a bit of an infiltrator,” Lawrence says. Red Rising is an infiltration story too. The story follows Darrow, described as “a rebel forged by tragedy, battling to lead his oppressed people to freedom.”

“It allows characters to really question themselves, cross weird moral boundaries and question the people around them. What happens when the people you hate suddenly become more relatable than some of the people that you’re fighting for?” he says. “I’ve always been fascinated by those kinds of stories.”

Also on deck is BioShock, an adaptation of 2K Games’ 2007 first-person shooter. “We’re doing a rewrite on a script now,” Lawrence says. “I think that’s getting closer and closer.” In fact, he had a call about the project scheduled for shortly after his conversation with THR.

Thirteen years after the Hunger Games franchise began, the odds remain firmly in Panem’s favor. In its opening weekend, Sunrise on the Reaping grossed $120 million, becoming its fourth-best debut in franchise history, besting 2015’s second Mockingjay movie ($102.6 million) and 2023 prequel The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes ($44.6 million) but behind 2013’s Catching Fire ($158 million), 2012’s series-launching The Hunger Games ($152.5 million) and 2014’s first Mockingjay ($121.8 million).

For Lawrence, who has lived in this world for more than a decade, the prequels have done more than extend the franchise. They’ve changed how he understands it.

“Going through each of these books page by page with Suzanne over time has given me a pretty deep knowledge of the world and the lore. But now that she’s writing these prequels and reframing characters, I’m also learning more each time she releases a book,” he says.

He counts himself a fan, but perhaps not the biggest one on set. “I’m sure there are super fans, even people like Mckenna Grace [Maysilee Donner in Sunrise], who is a super fan of these stories, who know more about all the little nooks and crannies than I do,” he says with a laugh.

After so many years of working side by side with Collins, the page and the screen have started to merge in his mind. “The lines between the book and the script start to blur, and you forget what’s just in the movies and what’s exactly from the book,” he says.

Plenty of dystopian YA series followed in The Hunger Games‘ wake, Divergent and The Maze Runner among them, but none has lasted the way Panem has. Lawrence credits Collins’ finger on the pulse of the world as the reason for the story maintaining its popularity.

“Part of what originally separated these books from other YA publications: they had real meat on the bone thematically,” Lawrence says of the story’s lasting appeal. “She also created a world, a narrative and characters people fell in love with: Katniss, Peeta, Effie Trinket. But if they didn’t have the ideas underneath them, they would feel a bit empty and surface-level, and they wouldn’t land the same way. That’s one of the things I feel really lucky about, honestly: we get to make movies that lots of people go to see, but that really have something to say about the world we live in. For me, that’s why they endure.”

Does telling this story in today’s climate feel different than it did when the original films came out? “A little bit,” Lawrence says. But the ideas, he argues, have always been built to last.

“Suzanne’s original inspiration was watching war footage, then switching channels and seeing reality game shows, and she decided to write this series about the consequences of war for teens. At the time, it was about an idea that was relevant in the moment but also timeless. That’s what she does: she looks at the world and finds ideas that are relevant but weirdly still timeless,” he says.

Whether Collins has more to say in Panem is a question only she can answer. In the meantime, the world she built keeps showing up in the real one. Fans dress as the characters at premieres, Lawrence notes, and the films’ songs and three-finger salute have even appeared at protests, “whether it’s Hungary, whether it’s Paris.”

“So much of what is created is so disposable,” he says, “and I feel like luckily Hunger Games is not.”
